Silicon Republic
NASA and IBM unveil open-source lunar AI model
2026-09-11

NASA and IBM unveil open-source lunar AI model

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
The AI model could help scientists better identify lunar ice, volcanos and craters. A new open-source AI model trained on NASA’s extensive lunar observation data is expected to help scientists better navigate moon colonisation plans. The NASA‑IBM Lunar Foundation Model is one of the first publicly available foundation models for the scientific exploration of the moon, coming just months after the two organisations’ Prithvi AI made history by becoming the first geospatial foundation model to be deployed in orbit. The Earth’s lunar neighbour has an arid and varied topography covered in craters, ice and volcanic activity – all picked up over the decades by various sensors and instruments. The petabytes of collected data, is, however, scattered across maps and images that need physical examination, or are accessible only by task-specific machine learning models. A domain-specific AI model could change how scientists analyse data, potentially accelerating how lunar geography is identified and understood. The Lunar Foundation Model achieves this by combining multimodal and multi-resolution observations. According to IBM and NASA, the new model can be used to investigate potential lunar ice deposits in the moon’s permanently shadowed regions, which could indicate the presence of water and oxygen. It could also be used to study lunar volcanic features, called ‘irregular mare patches’, and better identify craters to ensure safer landing sites for spacecraft. “NASA has spent decades building an extraordinary scientific record of the moon, but collecting data is only part of the job,” said Kevin Murphy, the chief science data officer and acting chief data and AI officer at NASA. “We also have to make data easier for scientists to explore and use. The NASA-IBM Lunar Foundation Model shows what’s possible when we bring AI to NASA’s petabytes of scientific data. That’s a real opportunity we see with AI – turning large-scale data into new discoveries.” Alongside the model, IBM and NASA also scientists built the first open-source lunar dataset of its kind, made out of layers of data from nine instruments used across four missions. The dataset includes tens of thousands of images and lunar surface maps from NASA’s Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter and the GRAIL mission, and data from the Japanese Aerospace Exploration Agency. Bending Spoons acquires Miro collaboration platform for $1.35bn

Bending Spoons acquires Miro collaboration platform for $1.35bn

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
This is Bending Spoons’ second acquisition since going public in July. Italy’s Bending Spoons, which buys and revamps existing businesses providing digital technologies, is acquiring California-based workplace collaboration platform Miro for $1.35bn. The deal values Miro at approximately $1.79bn in equity value, a huge step down from its last valuation of $17.5bn in 2022 – part of a downward trend attributed to the major AI-led disruption in the SaaS sector in recent years. The company, co-headquartered in Amsterdam, was founded in 2011 by Andrey Khusid and Oleg Shardin. Its platform, used by companies such as Ubisoft, PepsiCo and Comcast, allows workers to connect various tools and AI outputs into one governed location for collaboration. Miro’s customer base has grown multi-fold in recent years – up from 30m users in 2022 to more than 100m this year. The company recently recorded $600m in annual recurring revenue, with nearly all of it coming from its business and enterprise customers. Miro employs around 1,600 people in 13 hubs around the world. As part of the deal, a number of Miro shareholders have agreed to invest $295m into newly-issued Bending Spoons equity, the two companies said in a joint statement. “We acquire businesses with the intention of owning and operating them for the long term, and Miro will be no exception,” said Bending Spoons CEO and co-founder Luca Ferrari. “After closing, we plan to invest substantially in the fundamentals that its customers value: performance, reliability and functionality that supports critical collaborative work.” This is Bending Spoons’ second acquisition since going public in July. Last month, the company announced that it was acquiring US automation company Airtable at an implied equity value of around $2.25bn. The Italian company owns more than 50 brands, including Evernote, WeTransfer, Eventbrite and AOL. “Nearly 4m paying users depend on [Miro], across every industry and region we serve, including more than 750 customers with over $100,000 in annual recurring revenue,” said Khusid, CEO and co-founder of Miro. Bending Spoons shares gained marginally at market close yesterday. The company’s shares have been down nearly 23pc in the last month. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Hunter Biden’s $LAPTOP Meme Coin Is Either a National Disgrace or the Funniest Thing He’s Ever Done, and We’re Not Entirely Sure Which
2026-09-11

Hunter Biden’s $LAPTOP Meme Coin Is Either a National Disgrace or the Funniest Thing He’s Ever Done, and We’re Not Entirely Sure Which

Futurism | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. At this point, if you’re not using personal access to political power to run billion-dollar crypto scams, you’re seriously missing out. Previously, it was the Trump dynasty cashing in, using the family name to rake in over $1 billion off the backs of gullible crypto bros. Now, it’s former president Joe Biden’s son Hunter, whose memecoin $LAPTOP is following the tell-tale signs of a cyrpto rug pull. If you’re not familiar with this side of the cryptoverse, here’s a quick breakdown. A memecoin is basically an extremely volatile financial vehicle designed to cash in on trending memes or other viral phenomena. Unlike other cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin, memecoins lack even the thinnest pretense of real world utility. Instead, they live or die on hype alone, with the vast majority of memecoins collapsing like a house of cards as soon as a few early investors begin selling off their holdings. This, of course, is exactly what happened with $LAPTOP. According to DeCrypt, the young Biden’s memecoin launched at 8 am on Wednesday morning, peaking at a price of $190.81 dollars just two minutes after opening to traders. Before 9 am rolled around, the value of the memecoin had plummeted to just $3.70, collapsing from a fully diluted value of $144 billion to $4.8 billion in a matter of minutes. As the Washington Post observed, roughly 80 percent of people who bought the crypto token ended up losing money. Biden, for his part, touted $LAPTOP as a way to reclaim his honor in the wake of the convoluted laptop controversy of 2020, where it was alleged that the younger Biden was appropriating his father’s political position in business dealings in Ukraine and China. “The symbol they used to try to end me is now a symbol of resilience, redemption, and recovery,” Biden posted on X-formerly-Twitter before the memecoin went public. “They turned laptop into a weapon. I turned it into a token.” It’s unclear exactly how much he made from the rugpull, or where exactly any of that money went. In his post, Biden alluded to a “mechanism” by which “20 percent of the coins will be airdropped to the community,” without elaborating on what that means. A further portion of the tokens could be sent to a charity in a spiteful dig at Trump, as Biden cryptically explained. “Thirty percent of the tokens are tied to a pre-programmed mechanism,” he tweeted. “We publicly say what has to happen in the world. If it happens, the corresponding tokens are permanently burned. If it does not, those tokens are sent to charity. Either way, they leave our hands and an additional 50 million tokens will go to charity no matter what.” “With $TRUMP, nearly one million wallets have collectively lost ~$3.8 [billion],” Biden continued. “With my token, 20 percent of the coins will be airdropped to the community, and I am even including people that lost money on the $TRUMP grift.” A follow-up post published on the blogging platform Medium clarifies that this “mechanism” is a kind of prediction-market line, where “30 percent of supply is tied to public, verifiable events criteria.” “When a prediction resolves YES, the tokens are burned. When it doesn’t, they go to charity,” Biden explained. Basically, there are dozens of these “events” baked into $LAPTOP that determine what happens to the tokens as time goes on. Some of them are relatively straightforward, like “will President Trump be impeached during his term?” while others only add to the labyrinthine scheme. “The first event, 5,000,000 tokens, was resolved yesterday when Beeple referenced $LAPTOP,” Biden wrote on his blog. Of course, the memecoin’s valuation matters a great deal: whether the five millions tokens are priced at a few cents or a few hundred dollars when the prediction resolves has drastic implications for the final payout. Now that the price of LAPTOP is in the gutter, the not-at-all-ironclad law of memecoins says that it’s extremely unlikely to shoot back up without a major cash injection. In all, it’s an extremely sketchy — not to mention gimmicky — setup for a guy who’s already worth $17 million. Maybe it’d be one thing if all of the proceeds were guaranteed to end up with a charity, but that whole mechanism looks to be a distant afterthought. Instead, Biden is focused on the more nebulous task of “help[ing] each other and our country to get back up” — though how a hundred-billion-dollar crypto scam is supposed to accomplish that remains to be seen. It’s no secret that car owners have handed an immense amount of control over their vehicles to software. The situation become particularly harrowing for Sydney native Narghiza Ergashova, who watched helplessly as her former partner — who she says had already been abusing her for four years — started remotely controlling her Tesla from his smartphone. As The Guardian reports, police records detail her terrifying experience at the hands of her domestic abuser, 50-year-old businessman Enrico Pucci, last year. Ergashova found that Pucci had activated a “parental control” profile on the vehicle, going as far as to tail her in his own car as he yelled at her about her new partner. “You thought you’d get away with this,” he yelled, according to police records quoted by The Guardian. “I am in your car. I am in your maps.” While Ergashova technically owns the vehicle, Pucci had previously offered to “assist her” by using the accompanying Tesla app, giving him extensive control over the car. According to Tesla’s website, parental controls can be accessed via the accompanying mobile app and allow the user to change a long list of settings. It also allowed Pucci to bring the Tesla to a crawl at just 25 mph on main roads, for instance, by activating a “Valet Mode” intended to only be used by valets. That caused “significant safety issues for the victim on the road,” according to the police documents. Pucci also started setting the cabin temperature of the vehicle to extremely cold and hot temperatures and could even predetermine when the car could be driven by implementing curfew hours, features clearly intended for young learners. “The victim has not had any control of her vehicle since she noticed the parental control profile added on the 1st November 2025,” law enforcement noted. “The accused has been the only one with access to the vehicle’s controls utilising the Tesla app. This has caused the victim significant stress, mental harm and fears for her physical safety when operating her own vehicle.” Ergashova’s abuser has an extensive track record. Pucci has been convicted of 30 domestic violence-related offences, according to the Sydney Morning Herald, including eight counts of stalking, two counts of choking, one count of coercive control, and assault leading to bodily harm. He has also been convicted of creating revenge porn and theft. Fortunately, he has been brought to justice. Pucci pleaded guilty and was sentenced to two years in prison last week. Anthropic Just Revealed That It’s Stopped Foreign Agents From Using Claude to Develop Possible Bioweapons

Futurism | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Anthropic says it stopped several instances of foreign scientists using its AI agents to create possible biological weapons, the New York Times reports. The alarming news was revealed in a lengthy report released by the Claude chatbot-maker on Thursday. Notably, it comes amid heightened AI safety concerns after a former Anthropic employee accused the company of irresponsibly building powerful AI systems despite knowing they’re destructive. According to the report, scientists in several countries where Anthropic has blocked access to its models subverted these controls and tried to “obfuscate the purpose of their research to evade our safeguards.” In one case in May, a scientist asked Claude to help write an application to receive a state-sponsored grant for an eyebrow-raising project: to engineer more harmful mutations of the mosquito-borne chikungunya virus, which can cause fever and debilitating joint pain. The project, Anthropic believes, was intended to be carried out at a military research institute. “What we don’t know is if the research was meant to be weaponized,” Jacon Klein, Anthropic’s head of threat intelligence, told the NYT. “But a military institution doing gain-of-function research is concerning,” he added, referring to research that seeks to enhance or add to the biological capabilities of organisms and pathogens. The company documented four other similar cases where scientists evaded its guardrails to pursue shady biological research. It banned all of the offending accounts. But, as with the chikungunya virus case, Anthropic couldn’t definitively determine if these inquiries were part of legitimate research or an attempt to engineer bioweapons. It nonetheless chose to err on the side of caution. “You are not seeing someone in a comic book kind of way say, ‘Hey, I want to build a biological weapon to kill everybody,'” Klein told the NYT. “It’s an incredibly nuanced situation.” For years now, the AI industry has warned of the risk of AI being used to create bioweapons, including Anthropic’s CEO Dario Amodei. In a lengthy essay published earlier this year, Amodei vowed that the company was constantly improving its guardrails to prevent models from assisting with bioweapon research and opined that legislation and international cooperation was necessary to mitigate the risks. Still, while Anthropic claims to have thwarted these misuses of its models, the news casts doubt on whether it and other AI companies can guarantee the responsible use of its tech. By the company’s own admission, its safeguards failed, potentially leaving the door wide open for other bad actors in the future. In short, if these state actors were indeed looking to craft bioweapons, it’s a dramatic escalation, suggesting we’re edging closer to a catastrophe. That’s of course if general purpose models like Anthropic’s are capable enough to meaningfully assist these dangerous efforts; amid the heightened safety debate, the company has faced accusations of fear-mongering about its own tech as a marketing tactic. Some experts are of the mind that the threat is very much already real. Andrew Weber, a senior fellow on the Council on Strategic Risks who reviewed Anthropic’s report ahead of its release, told the NYT that its findings were “chilling examples of state-sponsored biological weapons developers tapping into the rapidly advancing capabilities” of frontier AI models. OpenAI’s Supposed Mathematical Breakthrough Devolves Into Explosive Drama as Mathematician Accuses It of Stealing His Work
2026-09-10

OpenAI’s Supposed Mathematical Breakthrough Devolves Into Explosive Drama as Mathematician Accuses It of Stealing His Work

Futurism | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. On Tuesday, OpenAI announced that it had found a solution for a famous unsolved problem in mathematics by using a swarm of advanced AI agents. But the occasion quickly unraveled into an explosive scandal, with mathematicians accusing the company of stealing their work. OpenAI’s purported solution was for one of the so-called Navier-Stokes equations, which mathematically describe how fluids, ranging from wind to ocean currents, move and behave. These differential equations — equations that describe how different quantities change in relation to others — have been used for nearly two centuries with reliable results, but scientists have long wondered if there are scenarios where their predictions break down and describe something that’s physically impossible. In 2000, the Clay Mathematics Institute listed the Navier-Stokes as one of its six Millennium Problems, with a prize of $1 million for being the first to solve each one. Enter OpenAI, which claims it found a way to break the Navier-Stokes by unleashing 10,000 of its AI agents on the problem for 88 hours, costing millions of dollars in computing costs, according to OpenAI’s head of research Mark Chen. The long and short of some exceedingly complicated math was that the legions of AI agents identified an apparent scenario in which the equations can describe fluids accelerating to an infinite velocity — resulting a so-called “blowup” or singularity. This, as far as we know, is impossible, thus demonstrating that the equations have a breaking point. But hours before OpenAI’s formal announcement came an explosive claim from the NYU mathematician Tristan Buckmaster, who had been working on the problem for years before OpenAI got involved, and was close to publishing his own solution. In a lengthy public statement, Buckmaster accused OpenAI’s solution of following a suspiciously similar path to his own, and said that the AI company only poured its resources into the Navier-Stokes after recently learning about the progress Buckmaster and his colleague Levent Alpöge, a mathematician at OpenAI’s biggest rival Anthropic, had been making. Adding to the suspicion, Buckmaster said he had been using OpenAI’s Codex tool to help check his work, insinuating that the company accessed his sessions, accidentally or intentionally, to copy it. When Buckmaster asked OpenAI about this, he didn’t get an answer, he claimed. OpenAI’s response to Buckmaster’s allegations only added fuel to the fire. While denying that its researchers and AI agents accessed or saw the mathematician’s work before it was publicly released, OpenAI also admitted that it “cannot rule out that de-identified data derived from their usage of our products helped improve our models.” That’s not all. Buckmaster also alleges that OpenAI mathematician Sébastien Bubeck pressured him to cut Alpöge as an author on his paper, which Buckmaster implies is because Alpöge worked for Anthropic. When Buckmaster refused and threatened to go public, Bubeck allegedly replied, “Why would you ruin your career?” Bubeck has denied Buckmaster’s version of events, calling them “false and inflammatory” in a public statement. The academic community has largely been appalled by what they’re seeing. Terence Tao, a renowned mathematician and leading authority on differential equations, excoriated what AI was doing to the field. “We have now seen that even the rumor of someone working on a problem can trigger a massive amount of AI-powered effort to flatten it before the original research project has time to reach its full potential,” Tao wrote on Mastodon. “The incentives may now be pointing in the direction of no longer sharing any promising research directions with the broader community, which would reverse centuries of traditions of open science and do serious long-term damage to the future of the field.” More on AI: OpenAI Denies Coverup After Rogue Swarm of Agents Reportedly Targeted a Second Site From Hugging Face

Though the math on this is pretty simple, the massive figure leaves more questions around the theoretical payout than answers. Either way, we’re talking about an 80 year old man who recently started believing he was lifted from the rubble of the World Trade Center collapse — which is to say there’s a pretty good chance he won’t remember his $1.3 trillion promise come November. LG TVs Caught Secretly Recording Users and Scanning Their Homes For Other Devices, Even When Disconnected From the Internet
2026-09-10

LG TVs Caught Secretly Recording Users and Scanning Their Homes For Other Devices, Even When Disconnected From the Internet

Futurism | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. It’s no exaggeration to say that if you own an LG smart TV, it might be spying on you. The widely respected computer hardware experts at the YouTube channel Gamers Nexus found that the company’s boob tubes and pricey OLEDs can scan your Wi-Fi network to search for the devices you own, secretly record audio logs of your conversations using built-in microphones, and keep tabs on what you’re watching and listening to. It collects all this data, Gamers Nexus found, even when the TV is disconnected from the internet and appears to be off in standby mode. To unearth these surreptitious practices, Gamers Nexus partnered up with independent security researchers and Level1Techs. Their resulting two-hour video report has since been picked up by other trusted cybersecurity voices like Malwarebytes. By digging into the firmware and intercepting some of the data the TVs were sending, the team found that the TVs were extensively scanning local Wi-Fi networks to log what devices were on it, including phones, computers, and other smart-home devices like thermostats. It even collected the names of nearby Wi-Fi networks, creating an alarmingly detailed picture of all the devices in a user’s household. Why might that information be relevant to LG? The TVs also use a controversial technology called automated content recognition (ACR) that routinely scans and determines what you’re watching so it can sell that data to advertisers. Combined together, they build an advanced and disturbingly comprehensive profile of what you watch and the devices you use, Gamers Nexus warn. All this data is sent back to LG Ad Solutions, its lucrative advertising business. LG TVs, on top of it all, can also listen to you. Tests showed that their built-in microphones could capture clean audio of nearby conversations and generate transcripts of them, even when the TVs were in standby mode, which most TVs default to when you think you’ve turned them off, with the display powering down. The recording feature continued even when the TVs were unplugged from the internet, storing the audio files locally and uploading them the moment the internet connection was restored. The researchers also found a vulnerability that allegedly allow a hacker to remotely execute code, though they’re keeping the details on this light so they can responsibly disclose their findings. Many LG owners are furious about the news. “LG hardware is just spyware,” fumed a post on the r/LGOLED subreddit. “You[r] TV is wiretapping your whole house.” Smart TVs collecting data on you isn’t new — ACR in particular is well-documented — but the reported findings here illustrate a degree of invasiveness the public could only speculate about previously. It’s far from the first time LG has caused a stir over purported privacy violations, either. Another Gamers Nexus investigation last month found that LG monitors were sneakily installing pop-up ads onto customers’ computers. The ensuing controversy caught Microsoft’s attention, with the company demanding LG remove the ads. More on surveillance: The Real Victims of the AI Surveillance Panopticon? Crime Fiction Writers

Scammers target hundreds of thousands of crypto owners after Trezor confirms data breach of email provider
2026-09-11

Scammers target hundreds of thousands of crypto owners after Trezor confirms data breach of email provider

TechCrunch | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Hardware crypto wallet maker Trezor is warning customers for the second time in as many months that one of the companies it relies on was hacked, exposing the data of Trezor’s customers to hackers. In a blog post this week, the hardware wallet maker said a cyberattack on Brevo, a marketing tech company that Trezor uses to send newsletters, allowed hackers to send around 347,000 phishing emails to Trezor customers with a malicious link purporting to come from the wallet maker. The link, when tapped, downloads an app that asks the victim for their wallet backup password. According to Trezor, one of the email subject lines said: “Critical Security Alert: STM32 Entropy Vulnerability.” With a stolen wallet password, a hacker can irreversibly steal the person’s funds on the public blockchain. Brevo said in an incident status post that the hackers were able to access 138 Brevo accounts to send out the mass volume of phishing messages. Brevo said that the hackers abused a flaw that meant the hackers’ access was “not properly scoped.” The company said that the hackers’ access was “wrongly granted” to all organizations that the hackers’ accounts could reach. The breach highlights a common security incident, where hackers compromise data held by third-party companies that are necessary for fulfilling orders or purchases from customers. Trezor says none of its products, wallets, or account system were affected by the incident. This is the second breach in recent weeks affecting Trezor, after the company alerted customers in August that one of its shipping partners was compromised in a data breach. The incident at the mailing company ShipMonk exposed the names, phone numbers, email addresses, and postal addresses of at least 81,000 people who bought and received Trezor wallet hardware. The data breach could put crypto owners and other wealthy individuals at risk of targeted violence and so-called “wrench” attacks, which rely on physical attacks to extract passwords from people. In the weeks following the breach at ShipMonk, some people have received letters by mail claiming to be from Trezor, featuring a QR code that, when scanned, opens up a fake page that attempts to steal the victim’s crypto wallet password. Trezor said it was reevaluating its relationships with its vendors and warned customers that their email addresses may be used again for future phishing attacks. Thrive Capital led VCs into pro sports ownership; Collaborative Fund just upped that play
2026-09-10

Thrive Capital led VCs into pro sports ownership; Collaborative Fund just upped that play

TechCrunch | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Collaborative Fund, the 15-year-old, New York-based generalist venture firm that has roughly $1 billion under management and that made early bets on Lyft, Reddit, Sweetgreen, and Olipop, among others, is taking a stake in the soccer club D.C. United and its stadium, Audi Field. It’s the latest — and smallest — firm to try something that Thrive Capital opened the door to just months ago: turning venture money into pro sports ownership. To recap, Joshua Kushner’s Thrive launched a new vehicle, Thrive Eternal, explicitly built to hold “iconic franchises and cultural institutions” for decades, funded by many of the same investors already in Thrive’s venture and growth funds. The firm kicked things off by announcing a stake in the San Francisco Giants. Months later, the same vehicle — with former Disney CEO Bob Iger, a Thrive partner, joining as co-owner — bought the Lakers outright for a record $12.5 billion. That’s new. Historically, money has poured into pro sports two other ways: individual tech fortunes and private equity. For example, Vinod Khosla and his family agreed this summer to buy the Seattle Seahawks for a record $9.6 billion soon after the Khosla family also took a stake in the San Francisco 49ers alongside OpenAI chairman Bret Taylor. That was a personal-wealth play, the kind we’ve seen over and over. Private equity firms have also been at this for years, including Sixth Street, which holds stakes in the Boston Celtics, the New England Patriots, and MLB’s San Francisco Giants; Ares, which owns a piece of the Miami Dolphins outright and separately financed Chelsea’s stadium plans through a $500 million preferred-equity deal; RedBird, which owns AC Milan outright and holds a minority stake in Fenway Sports Group, the holding company behind Liverpool and the Red Sox; and Arctos, with minority positions scattered across MLB, the NFL, the NBA, and European soccer. (Apollo, the newest entrant, has mostly stuck to sports financing deals so far rather than ownership stakes.) Thrive and Collaborative are doing neither of those things. At the same time, the two firms’ approaches to sports ownership look very different. Thrive built a stand-alone, permanent-capital vehicle specifically to hold trophy assets. Collaborative is investing out of the same early-stage fund it uses to write seed and Series A checks, and treating the deal less like something to buy and hold and almost more like infrastructure. In a memo shared with TechCrunch, Collaborative Fund founder and managing partner Craig Shapiro framed the deal as an extension of what the firm already does. “A franchise is the ultimate consumer product,” he wrote, arguing that D.C. United’s status as one of Major League Soccer’s original clubs gives Collaborative access to an institution with a decades-long fan base to build on. He pointed to the tailwinds around American soccer specifically (a World Cup just behind the sport, the LA Olympics ahead of it, soaring youth participation numbers in the U.S.), as well as D.C.’s ownership of Audi Field in Washington, D.C., plus a talent-development pipeline through Loudoun County, Virginia, and rights to a future Baltimore expansion team. Indeed, the thesis Shapiro laid out at a TechCrunch StrictlyVC event Thursday night in New York is less about owning a piece of an appreciating asset — the sports team itself — and more about what the team makes possible. Collaborative wants to turn Audi Field into what he describes as a living showcase for its own portfolio. As a backer of both fitness band maker Whoop and the beverage brand Olipop, for example, Collaborative Fund is imagining a Whoop wearables activation for fans, or Olipop drinks woven into game-day concessions. He’s thinking about the stadium’s foot traffic — tens of thousands of people showing up on a predictable schedule — as a distribution channel at a time when, because AI is making more of daily life feel synthetic, live experiences are becoming more valuable. Shapiro doesn’t dwell on this, but it surely helped sell Collaborative’s investors that team valuations have been soaring, so the stake could pay off on its own. Soccer valuations in particular have been on a tear. Inter Miami’s franchise value has roughly doubled in the two years since Lionel Messi arrived, MLS’s average club value is up roughly 134% since 2019, and D.C. United’s own valuation has climbed from $35 million in 2008 to $785 million today, factoring in its ownership of Audi Field and the surrounding real estate. If Shapiro is right that a franchise is also “the ultimate consumer product,” it could be a pretty good place to park money. Time will tell. Jensen Huang explains why Nvidia will grow an astounding 70% next year
2026-09-10

Jensen Huang explains why Nvidia will grow an astounding 70% next year

TechCrunch | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Founder, CEO, and tireless Nvidia hype man Jensen Huang told attendees at the Goldman Sachs Communacopia + Technology conference on Thursday why his company’s AI domination — and revenues — will continue its record-breaking growth streak through the end of next year. There’s been endless hand-wringing over whether Nvidia’s party will end as it faces increasing competition for GPUs and AI chips from all directions: the hyperscalers (Amazon, Microsoft, and Google, each building their own) and the AI labs (Anthropic and OpenAI, which are building their own), as well as from newly public competitor Cerebras and startups such as Etched. “Most people think Nvidia builds a chip. I mean, you need airplanes to ship what we build,” Huang said, adding that the company continues to battle a perception from its early days. Nvidia invented the GPU, which back then were largely sold to consumers to improve PC gaming. “One GPU now is not $399. It’s $8.5 million dollars. That’s one GPU, all connected with NVLink, 2 million parts, right? 250,000 kilowatts. That’s a GPU, and we ship thousands of them.” He added that orders for just one product, a computer system that combines 36 Grace CPUs with 72 Blackwell GPUs, is currently experiencing 27% month-to-month sales growth. Huang didn’t limit his bullish view to current sales though. He took the opportunity to reiterate Nvidia’s revenue outlook for next year — guidance the company provided last month when it reported yet another record-breaking revenue quarter. That’s when he first said revenue could grow by 70% next year. “I think we could grow 70% year over year. We’re confident about that,” Huang said again on Thursday. Analysts expect the company to end its current fiscal year at about $400 billion in revenue. So 70% growth would mean around $680 billion next year. Huang explained why he’s confident: His company is so embedded in every area of AI that he believes he can see the future. “Nvidia runs every model. Every single lab can use us,” the CEO said, mentioning that this includes models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google, as well as open-weight offerings. “We are a foundational platform of the AI ecosystem, foundational platform of the AI industry.” Nvidia’s fingers extend all the way from its suppliers, such as memory chip makers, to data center projects and startups. “We’re tracking every single gigawatt of land, power, shell around the world. Literally everything on the planet,” he said. (“Shell” refers to the shell of a data center building before it is outfitted with computers). “I mean, just think about all my partners. How many neoclouds are reporting back to us? How many OEMs are reporting back to us? How many clouds are reporting back to us? How many AI-native companies are reporting back to us? We’re working with everybody, and so we kind of know where everything is,” he said. That comment led to inevitable questions about Nvidia’s so-called circular deals, in which it invests in companies that turn around and buy its wares. Such schemes famously contributed to the downfall of a previous generation of internet build-out suppliers like Lucent Technologies. Huang had a simple, albeit cheeky, response. “Well, it’s not circular because we put a little bit of money in, and a lot of money comes back.” He went on to joke, “I look at the spreadsheet, we put in $1 and $100 comes back in. Is that circular? If that is, let’s do more of that.” Quips aside, he insisted that before any of these companies gets an investment, Nvidia makes sure it has real contracts generating revenue from customers. All told, he said he’s seen $100 billion worth of such contracts, “I’m not taking any risks. … I need a sure thing.” Time will tell whether Nvidia’s stronghold on AI can really persist long term. If there’s one golden rule of the tech industry, it’s that all big things get disrupted. Right now, even Huang admits that much of AI’s growth is coming from AI-native startups that are raising vast sums and spending most of that cash on their own AI use. As the AI industry matures, expect companies to become more efficient in how they use infrastructure and tokens. But for now, Nvidia has its finger in every pie and sees another year of plenty in its future. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. OpenAI puts Pro subscriptions on hold due to Astra demand

TechCrunch | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Demand for OpenAI’s newest and most powerful model, Astra, has led the company to temporarily pause subscriptions for its $200-per-month Pro plan, citing strain on its infrastructure. The move was announced on X by OpenAI’s product leader, Thibault (Tibo) Sottiaux, who leads core products like Codex and ChatGPT at the AI lab. He said that the Pro plan puts the most strain on its systems, which is why sign-ups for this service tier are now being disabled. “We wanted to take the smallest step that allows us to continue giving the broadest access possible,” Sottiaux wrote. He added that the company’s other plans, including the API and lower-cost Go and Plus plans, remain available. The company warned on Wednesday that such a change might come. At the time, Sottiaux wrote, “Demand for Astra is really unprecedented. We’re pulling all the levers possible to sustain the demand, but I’ve not seen anything like it until now and we went through very steep growth before. Priority will always be to keep excellent service for existing users, but we might have to pause new Pro subscriptions for a bit if this continues.” OpenAI hasn’t said how long sign-ups to the Pro tier may be disabled, or how many people are signing up daily, to give an idea of the scale of the demand. The company raised usage limits for Codex users as recently as last month, suggesting the strain is a recent phenomenon. Launched on September 3, Astra has been rolling out across OpenAI’s plans, including Pro, Plus, Enterprise, and Business accounts, in addition to its AI. The model promises a major leap forward in areas like AI reasoning, coding, and computer use — all areas of steep competition. OpenAI even heralded Astra as the beginning of the “AGI era,” and a generational leap, stoking demand even more. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Why a Divided BRICS Poses No Challenge to America
2026-09-11

Why a Divided BRICS Poses No Challenge to America

TIME | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A journalist reports from the venue of the upcoming BRICS Summit at the Bharat Mandapam in New Delhi on September 11, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings When the leaders of BRICS, the bloc of emerging economies, gather in New Delhi on Saturday for their 18th summit, they will have plenty to talk about. The group began with Brazil, Russia, India and China, then added South Africa in 2010. It has since expanded to include 11 countries. The leaders and diplomats of BRICS member states are meeting in New Delhi to consider how their combined political and economic muscle might be used to build an alternative to the American-led global order. In some ways, President Donald Trump’s refusal to allow America play its traditional postwar leadership role has created new opportunities for the bloc. Yet its ambitions remain constrained by an inconvenient fact: India, China, Russia and Brazil do not align particularly well. Here are the dynamics to watch this weekend. India, the host, wants the summit to accomplish as much as possible without antagonizing the United States. India continues to value the stability of its relationship with Washington, particularly as a counterbalance to China’s expanding influence in Asia and beyond. India, along with Brazil, has also resisted Russian and Chinese calls for a common BRICS currency and new lending to sanctions-hit Russia. For its part, China is seeking to extend its global influence. For President Xi Jinping, this summit is another stage in an international roadshow that has included a leading role at this year’s Shanghai Cooperation Organization gathering in Kyrgyzstan, a visit to Egypt to help mediate the ongoing war in the Middle East and, later this month, a state visit to the White House. In Delhi, Xi hopes to present China as a stable, predictable alternative to American influence. He will also seek to erode the dollar’s continuing dominance as the world’s principal reserve currency by promoting more channels for nondollar lending, the wider use of the renminbi to settle trade, and progress toward linking digital payment systems, an effort India has long resisted. Given the sometimes violent border clashes between China and India in recent years, observers will also be watching Xi’s and Modi’s body language closely. Despite their differences on key agenda items, they are likely to be all smiles. Neither has an interest in airing grievances. India, as host, wants a smooth show. China, which will host next year’s summit, also wants a positive atmosphere. And Xi’s overriding goal is to present his country as a mature and constructive global leader. Russia, of course, wants whatever diplomatic, political, and economic support it can get for its stalemated war with Ukraine. Brazil, meanwhile, hopes to forge new economic linkages and reaffirm its commitment to being a full BRICS partner, even as some fear that a Flavio Bolsonaro victory in next month’s presidential election could undermine the country’s commitment to the bloc. The founders will not be the only delegations in attendance. Iran, Saudi Arabia, and the United Arab Emirates, all new members, will be there, introducing fresh tensions into the agenda and some awkward photo calls. Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman and the UAE’s leader Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan, however, are expected to dispatch lesser officials in their place. The central question is how the summit’s final communiqué will address the war. The recent Shanghai Cooperation Organization summit produced a final document that included implicit criticism of the United States and Israel. We will see whether the UAE delegation allows for similar language from Delhi. Whatever emerges from the gathering, BRICS is unlikely to become an anti-American or anti-Western power bloc anytime soon. Its clout is already limited by the rivalry between China and India. The four founding members also have divergent interests whenever a conflict carries major consequences for energy markets: Russia and Brazil are major exporters who want higher energy prices while China and India, as major importers, prefer lower ones. Equally important, the bloc now includes American treaty allies or longstanding partners, among them the UAE, Saudi Arabia and Indonesia. Their presence will impose another check on BRICS’s ambitions. All of them want to hedge against dependence on an increasingly unpredictable America. Most of them also want to preserve a stable relationship with the global superpower.
TIME
How the Bold and Bloody True Story Behind The Uprising Reverberates Through History
2026-09-11

How the Bold and Bloody True Story Behind The Uprising Reverberates Through History

TIME | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The wind reaches the fens of eastern England before the light does, moving over the marshes in long gray sweeps. Andrew Garfield, muddied and short of breath, labors through it: a farmer with his ox, cutting furrows into the land. His character, Ploughman, is a man who refers to himself simply by what he does. Grief has pared him down to labor and memory. The plague took his wife and children, and all that remains is a hut, the animal that shares it through the winter for warmth, and the daily toll of survival. It’s slow and thankless work, the kind a kingdom takes for granted as readily as the earth beneath its feet. That lone farmer anchors Paul Greengrass’ new film The Uprising, in theaters Sept. 11. Written and directed by Greengrass, the filmmaker’s deepest reach into the past yet dramatizes the Peasants’ Revolt of 1381, when tens of thousands of English commoners, taxed past endurance in the long shadow of the Black Death, marched on London to demand a fairer world. Around Garfield, Greengrass assembled a cast that includes Jamie Bell as the rebel priest John Ball, Cosmo Jarvis as the revolt’s military leader Wat Tyler, Thomasin McKenzie as Alyce, a fictional follower of Ball, Woody Norman as the boy king Richard II, and Katherine Waterston as the Countess, Richard II’s mother. The film follows its hero from fens to road and road to London, then London toward the forest—as private grief grows into collective fury and fury reaches the gates of power. Beneath the armor and beyond the crowds, Greengrass’ epic hinges on the moment when people long treated as expendable begin to act on their claim to dignity and agency. Greengrass invented Ploughman for the film, but the forces pressing in on him are no invention. The director opted not to build The Uprising around one of the revolt’s historical figures, such as Tyler or Ball. “I didn’t want my central character to be a soldier or a priest,” he tells TIME. “I wanted him to be emblematic of all the tens of thousands of people who rose up. They were all ordinary farmers, ordinary people who lived in villages and small towns, who’d had enough.” That decision turns a vast uprising into the story of one man whose loss forces him to confront the larger indignities of the world that remains. Garfield recognized that feeling across centuries. “I think that everyone watching will feel that connection,” he says. “A kind of sense of: What do you do in the face of such grave injustice? How do we heal? How do we fight for our right to live a life of freedom and of dignity, and of connection?” Tom Hollander as Treasurer Hales, Woody Norman as King Richard II and Katherine Waterston as The Countess Courtesy fo Focus Features The Black Death reached England in the summer of 1348, most likely aboard ships that came into the Dorset port of Melcombe. Moving through the country at an estimated one to five miles a day, it reached London by November and, within two years, killed as much as half of England’s population. In its wake, whole villages were left empty. The horror was intimate and unrelenting: households vanished, kin buried kin, and parishes lost the priests who once baptized and married their people. Fields went to weed, and livestock wandered untended; the familiar rhythms of rural life gave way to an eerie void. The plague also changed the terms on which the survivors lived. Labor became scarcer, and scarce labor has a price. “What life could be is probably at the heart of this whole story,” says Dan Jones, the historian whose 2009 book Summer of Blood remains a defining account of the revolt. “Basic economics tell us that if you lose half the people, wages should go up. Rent should come down. Life for survivors ought, in theory, to get better.” Those left standing had reason to believe that life might improve. That possibility ran up against what Jones calls the “vested interests of power structures.” In England, he says, the government “immediately passes laws to suppress wages”: It imposed a maximum wage and sent judges into the countryside to enforce it. Mass death had made the old hierarchy newly fragile, and Parliament moved to restore it, with the laborer kept in his place and the landowner’s command protected. Then came the demands of war. England’s long conflict with France, a struggle over disputed territory as well as English claims to the French crown, continued, ruinous as ever, even as the plague cut the tax base and diminished the country’s productive power. The crown’s answer was a series of poll taxes, imposed on a population already strained by loss, labor restrictions, and military levies. There is, Jones says, “a class analysis to this rebellion”: elites were “taking aim squarely at the ordinary people,” first preventing them from improving their lot and then taxing them in ways they had not faced before. By the spring of 1381, deprivation gave way to imagination. People who ached for a better life were being ordered back into the old one, by statute and tax collector alike. The revolt drew force from a realization that threatened every hierarchy above it: the social order was made by people, and people could change it. Thomasin McKenzie as Alyce, Jamie Bell as John Ball, and Andrew Garfield as Ploughman Courtesy of Focus Features It began at the end of May, when villagers in Essex turned on the commissioners sent to collect the tax from them yet again. Within days, the uprising spread to Kent. On June 7, Tyler, likely a veteran of the French wars, emerged at the head of the Kentish rebels, who freed Ball, who had been jailed for seditious preaching, from the Archbishop’s prison at Maidstone; by June 10, they entered Canterbury. The couplet most famously associated with Ball put the rebellion’s challenge in a single question: “When Adam delved and Eve span, who then was the gentleman?” In a kingdom that understood rank as God’s design, the question was revolutionary. Adam worked the soil and Eve spun thread; no aristocrat stood beside them. So who, the couplet asks, gave one class the right to rule another? Jones describes Tyler and Ball as the revolt’s two hands, one holding the sword and the other the scriptures. Tyler brought military organization; Ball gave the movement its moral argument. “Between them, they create a very potent movement, because it’s not only organized but it’s radicalized,” Jones says. The name history settled on undersells the people who rose up. “It’s a very broad coalition,” Jones says, and the term “Peasants’ Revolt,” a historian’s term, can “obscure just what a wide range of people were involved.” This was not a rising merely of the utterly destitute. Whole villages moved with their structures intact: local leaders, parish priests trusted with their neighbors’ souls, craftsmen, and laborers, were joined in the capital by Londoners keeping their own accounts of unfairness. By Jones’ estimate, the coalition’s grievances ran through some 85% of the country, aimed at a thin sliver at the top—not at the king, whom they claimed as their own, but at the advisers, bishops, aristocrats, and officials they believed corrupted the realm. Women were certainly among the rebels. The surviving records place them at critical moments, including the storming of the Tower of London. Jones calls the revolt a “truly popular movement,” one that did not require its participants to possess the formal power otherwise held by men in a patriarchal society. What happened inside the city is the part no telling can sanitize. The rebels threw open the prisons and set fire to the New Temple, a Thames-side compound where lawyers-in-training lived and kept the books and records that helped sustain the kingdom’s legal order. They breached the Tower itself, where the king sheltered, dragged out the two men they blamed for the tax, Archbishop Simon Sudbury and Treasurer Robert Hales, and beheaded them. Jones contends the revolt demands two judgments at once. The grievances were real, and the people were being exploited. But the rising also became brutal, with violence extending beyond royal officials to others who were perceived as enemies of the revolt but had no hand in the tax. “You have to say that this was a good cause, and the rebellion got hideously out of control,” he says. By midsummer, the revolt reached its breaking point. Richard II was 14, a boy raised from infancy to believe he was fated to save the realm. On June 14, he rode out to meet the rebels and granted their demands, the abolition of serfdom among them. At Smithfield the following day, Tyler was killed during a parley with the king’s entourage. Richard calmed the crowds by declaring they would have no leader but him, then turned against the rising. Within weeks, he canceled the freedom charters granted in London. His government hunted down the movement’s leaders and executed them, and the king is said to have told the petitioners that serfs they were and serfs they would remain, on terms harsher than before. There is a catch in knowing this version of events. Many chroniclers were churchmen, and the church was among the rebels’ targets. The legal records are rich, Jones says, but were created from the “perspective of the suppression of the rebellion.” Historians, he says, “have to read against the sources.” The archive preserves the rebellion mostly as seen by those who feared or punished it. Garfield and the rebels Courtesy of Focus Features The Uprising grew from an ambition Greengrass held for years. The filmmaker behind United 93, Captain Phillips, The Lost Bus, and three of the Bourne films had long wanted to make a historical epic in the tradition of Gladiator and Braveheart. The material found him during the COVID-19 pandemic. He began writing on the second day of lockdown, when there were no vaccines and no way of knowing how far the crisis would spread. The last pandemic on that scale led him back to the 14th century. By the time Jones first met with Greengrass, a map of London during the rebellion was already hanging in the director’s office. Soon, the filmmaker says, the period felt “eerily contemporary.” The script’s first title shows how early the film had ties to the Robin Hood legend. “When I wrote it in lockdown, it was called The Hood because of the ending,” he says. Ploughman begins as a farmer, but his journey ends beyond the reach of the king’s law, in the forest. That turn places him beside an outlaw tradition already circulating in the late 14th century. William Langland’s Piers Plowman, the poem that helped inspire Greengrass’ protagonist, contains the earliest known written reference to Robin Hood, evidence that tales of the outlaw were already familiar before the surviving ballads were committed to writing. Jones sees the Peasants’ Revolt and medieval outlaw tales, including stories of Robin Hood, as products of the same political world: one in which ordinary people saw legal authority serving the powerful rather than protecting them. The early Robin Hood was no polished folk hero, Jones says, but a violent, devout yeoman pushed to society’s edge by corrupt officials. Its central tension was the “fundamental difference between law and justice.” As Greengrass puts it, at a time when people “felt powerless and betrayed,” they told stories “about outlaws who stood up to power when they couldn’t.” That rich idea shaped Greengrass’ choice of hero. Tyler and Ball could have supplied the film with a soldier and a priest, two historical figures already established in the record. Instead, the filmmaker begins with a farmer at the first spark in Essex, then follows him as the uprising gathers people, reaches London, and begins to turn against itself. The character is invented, but he comes from the revolt’s own language. Jones notes that actual letters found on Ball after his arrest, written to sustain the rebellion’s spirit, appeal to archetypal figures of the working land, including ploughmen like the one Garfield plays. Garfield felt the pull of his role at once. “It’s not often that I will read something and fight to play that part, and I did that with Paul,” he says. His note arrived after years of halting development, from an actor Greengrass knew only casually. “It’s the email you dream of getting from a brilliant actor who says, ‘I’ve read your movie. I really want to be in it,’” Greengrass says. To prepare, Garfield began with the rhythms Ploughman would know. “It’s a man that lives in a very, very different time and in a very, very different rhythm to me,” he says. “Connection to the earth and connection to nature, that felt very, very important.” He researched the seasons, harvests, clothing, daily labor, and the habits of the ox. Before filming, he spent time in Ploughman’s hut with the animal, learning the terms of a household in which the ox slept indoors through winter to provide warmth. “Connection to his ox, that was the primary relationship in the story,” Garfield says. The animal is at once Ploughman’s companion, source of heat, and fellow laborer. His wife and children are dead before the film begins, but Garfield imagined the life Ploughman lost so he could carry that grief across the film. He developed their history and their absence until they became, as he puts it, the “kind of invisible visible spirit characters that are there throughout the story.” Their absence helps explain why the character continues to seek justice, even after the uprising begins to lose its way. “They are the characters that are drawing him forward throughout the action of the film,” Garfield says. That loss follows Ploughman beyond the hut and into his encounter with Richard II. He was taught that the king speaks with God’s authority. When the crown proves capable of betrayal, Garfield sees the break as spiritual as much as political. “It’s like waking up from a cult,” he says. “It’s like waking up from being manipulated and misled and brought down a path that was being governed by evil, power-hoarding, oppression, being disguised as a kind of spirituality.” Greengrass places Ploughman’s awakening beside Richard’s. “Here’s this powerless farmer who is struggling to find agency in the world,” he says, and opposite him is “this 14-year-old boy king” trying to find agency of his own. Their paths cross, then diverge. “One goes from being the farmer to the outlaw, and the other goes from being the powerless king to the tyrant.” Outside Ploughman’s hut, Greengrass worked to establish the larger world that makes that private life so vulnerable. “Deep period movies have quite a close relationship with science fiction,” he says. “They’re both really cinematic world creations.” Greengrass uses costumes to mark the division between court and country. The period’s sumptuary laws regulated dress by rank, especially the cost and luxury materials people could wear. In the film, Ploughman and the people around him move through a restricted color palette; Richard II’s court does not. With little of medieval London still standing, Bavaria offered spaces that could be more readily adapted for the film’s 14th-century settings. At Penzing Studios, the open-air museum at Bad Windsheim, the fortress at Lichtenau, and sites in Nuremberg, the production found medieval stonework, corridors, chapels, crypts, and staircases suited to the royal court. St. Sebaldus Church, built in the 13th century, became several locations within the film. Constructed sets and visual effects widened those spaces. The London uprising scenes required the opposite approach: not courtly rooms and corridors, but night shoots packed with bodies, smoke, fire, and a city coming apart. Garfield does not think of the German supporting artists who stayed through the shoot as simply “extras.” “These supporting actors were the vital ingredient of this project,” he says. “It was a really profound experience to share that march and those fights with the same group of people every day.” Greengrass moved that company through a handful of locations, recomposing them for each setup until a modest crowd read on screen as a revolt spilling across the capital, and shot the scenes in firelight, with takes that could last 15 or 20 minutes. Within those long takes, Garfield had to keep track of his character, what he saw, and how he responded as the crowd shifted around him. The firelit crowds give the film its largest scenes. But more critical than the technical logistics of these shoots was posing the questions that arose from their aftermath. What remained, the film asks, after Richard II effectively took London back and the revolt ended?. Woody Norman as King Richard II Courtesy of Focus Features By summer’s end of 1381, the rising was broken: Tyler dead, Ball captured and executed, and the freedom charters canceled. Measured against its concrete demands, the revolt failed almost completely. But the decades that followed tell a more complicated story. Serfdom, the institution the rebels once asked their king to abolish, withered; by 1450, Jones says, when England’s next great rising came under Jack Cade, it was effectively finished, and the people who marched that time were more politically fluent than their grandparents. Greengrass sees in 1381 a distant beginning for the individual rights that developed over centuries. Jones brings the historian’s longer view to that reading. “Straight lines can only be drawn through history with retrospect and with the blinkers on,” he says. “But this is a moment when the ordinary people step onto the political stage in the English-speaking world, and they are never thereafter off it.” Greengrass’ ambivalence about such moments in history stems from his early work as a documentarian, when he filmed upheaval in Northern Ireland, apartheid South Africa, and other places where political orders were breaking apart. “Revolutions always eat themselves,” he says. “They start off with people’s intense hopes and dreams, but once you pull authority down, the result is chaos, and it’s only later, when it’s all over, that you can draw a ledger.” How to weigh that ledger, Greengrass leaves to the audience: gripped, he trusts, by Ploughman's journey, recognizing something of their own restless present in its medieval setting, and free to draw their own conclusions. The man who ends the film in the forest is, for Garfield, the man he always was: an invisible person, the kind who passes unseen through an ordinary day as “someone who simply wants to live a life of truth and dignity.” “I think that we can all identify with that in a deep way, in a kind of core way," the actor says. He hopes audiences, once they see the film, will notice the invisible people around them, and the invisible parts of themselves that ask only for small liberties: wind on the face, a dance every once in a while, a walk amid what he calls the "hard work of being alive.” That invisibility is rooted in centuries of historical silence. The archive remembers the crown, the church, and the officials who repressed the uprising more fully than it preserves the villagers who made it possible. Nearly everything that survives of the rebels comes down through their enemies’ pens; Greengrass’ film reaches toward lives that history left largely unseen. And while the revolt was put down in a distant summer, the claim beneath it—to dignity and agency—was not. It outlived the gallows, the revoked charters, and the chroniclers who reduced the people to a mob, and it endures in a handful of letters attributed to Ball urging the ploughmen of England to hold fast, written as if their author knew that in some inky field, one of them would look up. With The Uprising, Greengrass made a film about the man who does.

25 Years After 9/11, Americans Must Remember What Binds Us
2026-09-11

25 Years After 9/11, Americans Must Remember What Binds Us

TIME | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings An American flag and the 9/11 Memorial & Museum in front of One World Trade Center on September 7, 2026, in New York City. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings I’m almost certain I’ve heard more 9/11 stories than anyone else on earth. For almost 12 years, I served as the founding CEO of the National 9/11 Memorial & Museum, a role that invited countless strangers to share their stories from that terrible day. On planes, at gas stations, in meetings or on street corners, the mere mention of my work unlocked solemn reminiscing, and I always felt honored to listen. Over time, I have noticed a pattern. The stories typically begin on 9/11, but rarely end there. They drift into the weeks and months that followed: people who tossed duffel bags into their cars and drove hundreds of miles to volunteer at Ground Zero; sons and daughters compelled to enlist to fight in the names of the innocent victims; strangers who welcomed stranded travelers into their homes. Americans channeled grief and fear into acts of service and unity of purpose. I’ve always found it telling that even years after the attacks, these are the memories people chose to share with me. Tempting though it is to recall pre-9/11 America through sunny filters, America seethed with partisan animus even then. Nine months before the attacks, the Supreme Court had decided Bush v. Gore; three years earlier, the Clinton impeachment had rocked the country. But in the wake of 9/11, the chasms we once shouted across became neighborhoods again. It’s not that Americans suddenly agreed on all that divided them. And to be sure, we must continue to address the hate that some wrongly embraced in the aftermath of that tragic day. But 9/11 largely clarified an essential truth: beneath our disagreements lay common cause. The 2,977 people murdered that day hailed from 92 nations. They were rich and poor, religious and secular, native-born, and immigrant. They practiced different faiths, subscribed to different political views, and lived very different lives from even those seated right next to them on that fateful day. But in those differences, they represented the American experiment itself, and in putting aside our conflicts, Americans tacitly acknowledged that it was worth protecting. Twenty-five years later, I am alarmed not by the solidarity we’ve lost, but by the erosion of consensus about the terms under which we disagree. Democracy itself has become debatable, especially among young people. Only 66% of Americans now say democratically elected government is “extremely” or “very important” to the nation’s identity, down from 80% in 2021. Another recent survey found roughly one in five Americans endorse harassment, doxxing, or even violence against their political adversaries. And perhaps most stunning of all is that only 14% of those between the ages of 18 and 34 are extremely proud to be an American. Those figures belie a disturbing trend in our political discourse: moral absolutism. A healthy democracy makes space even for fierce dissent. The danger comes when political clashes become crusades, and the self-declared righteousness of the cause becomes license to abandon democratic restraints. Almost nothing went uncontested while we built the 9/11 Memorial & Museum. We fought over how to arrange the names of the dead, how and whether to represent the hijackers, and what would we delve into about America’s response. These weren’t academic debates. They were real conflicts waged by people of all backgrounds, laden with grief and anger, bound by deeply held personal, emotional, and political convictions and competing ideas about what America owed the dead and the living. Yet we still had to build on sacred ground. The experience taught me that pluralism doesn’t require agreement. It requires something harder: accepting that sometimes you will lose the argument, but you stay at the table anyway. Our job wasn’t to make disagreements disappear. There was no universe where that was possible. Instead, my job was to help determine which differences the institution had an obligation to accommodate and where it had an obligation to draw a line. We could tell the truth about American failures without concluding that America itself was a failure. We could explain why the terrorists hated this country without adopting their indictment of it. A democracy depends on the same distinctions. The rule of law, free expression, and equal citizenship are not partisan preferences to be renegotiated after every election. They are the terms of the deal. Protecting someone’s right to reject the American experiment is different from treating that rejection as simply another interpretation of it. A pluralistic society can tolerate almost limitless disagreement precisely because there are some principles it cannot and should not surrender. Now, this 25th anniversary demands more from us. The America attacked that morning was never defined by consensus. It was defined by a shared conviction that our freedoms belong as fully to people we bitterly oppose as to people we love; that our democracy is worth defending not because it settles our differences, but because it gives us a way to live with them; and that while each of us may nurture our own vision for a more perfect union, it remains a union nonetheless. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.
