How 9/11 Helped Shape Our Politics Today
2026-09-11

How 9/11 Helped Shape Our Politics Today

TIME | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The World Trade Center twin towers with Staten Island in the background in New York, New York, August 5, 1972. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings From the moment they occurred, the terror attacks of  Sept. 11, 2001 were seen as inaugurating a new age of asymmetric violence and warfare. President George W. Bush described the war on terror that followed as a generational conflict. Yet, for all the transformations it produced, Al-Qaeda and Islamic militancy more broadly have, in my view, receded within a decade. Not so much because it was defeated on the battlefield but because it was unable to gain much support among Muslims. When popular uprisings swept the Arab world in the early 2010s, protesters in Tunisia, Egypt, Syria, Bahrain, and elsewhere in the region took to the streets not in the name of Islam, but democracy. The Arab Spring, more than any military victory, signaled the decline of militancy. Al Qaeda faced its greatest defeat at Tahrir Square. Islamic militancy flourished principally on the battlefields of war on terror, in countries such as Afghanistan and Iraq, a trajectory epitomized by the emergence of the Islamic State from within the American prison system in Iraq. While Islamic militancy still survives in pockets of weak or fractured states, elsewhere it has petered out into the province of petty criminals or psychologically disturbed individuals. What, then, does its brief and brutal career mean for world history? The dramatic arrival of Al-Qaeda and Islamic militancy in the global public sphere in the early 2000s did not mark the beginning of a new chapter of global history. Perhaps that confrontation between Al-Qaeda and America signaled a historical ending: the true conclusion of the Cold War. By the fall of 2001, the Soviet Union and the bipolar world had been dead for a decade. The Cold War survived in the institutions and practices it had created: NATO, the United Nations and its conventions, international courts and the multilateral institutions established to knit together a divided world through systems of deterrence, development and compromise born of the fear of an atomic holocaust. For a while, these institutions appeared capable of operating without their founding cause, but that hope proved illusory. In the 1990s, wars swept across the Balkans, the Caucasus, the Middle East and Africa. Al-Qaeda arose at the confluence of post-Cold War globalization and unipolar violence. With its emergence at the turn of the millennium, Al-Qaeda, I argue, brought the Cold War to a definitive close by overturning its central presuppositions. The war on terror rendered the familiar categories of the Cold War—the balance of power, deterrence, great-power rivalry, and hegemony—increasingly obsolete. Fought in civilian spaces against nonstate actors dispersed around the globe, the war on terror also ended up destroying most of the Cold War’s remaining ideological states in the Middle East, including the socialist republics of Iraq, Libya, and Syria. Al-Qaeda's emergence also marked the end of Islamism, which was itself a Cold War movement dedicated to revolution and the creation of ideological states. Islamism’s only durable strongholds, Iran and Afghanistan, came to embody its takeover by clerical establishments of the kind it had once sought to displace. The war on terror also weakened the liberal state, another creature of the Cold War, whose animating purpose had been to pose capitalist liberty against Communist dictatorship. Osama bin Laden used to claim that Westerners thought their interventions in the Middle East would remake the region’s states in the images of their own, but instead it was the Americans and Europeans who came to resemble their despotic and corrupt clients in the region. One of the goals of Al-Qaeda’s campaign of terror was to precisely achieve that by showing how even violence falling short of a conventional war and posing no genuine existential threat was sufficient in provoking Western democracies to dismantle the civil liberties of their own citizens, exposing their claims to freedom as hypocritical. As the war on terror dragged on, Al-Qaeda’s leaders portrayed the anti-terror laws and the increasingly militarized conduct of the U.S. and its allies not as signs of resolve and strength but as displays of weakness and proof of the shallowness of Western liberalism. Bin Laden might have been disappointed by Al-Qaeda’s inability to mobilize enough Muslims around the world to overthrow their dictatorships, but the U.S. and its European allies, which sustained those authoritarian regimes, sadly behaved much as he had predicted. A sacrificial, if not nihilistic, movement whose iconic figure was the suicide bomber, Al-Qaeda had little interest in conquest and government. One of its major objectives was to provoke Western states into attacking their own democracies, in a kind of auto-immune response to terrorism, in which the effort to defend the political body corroded it from within. In that particular sense, bin Laden was perversely successful in dragging America and its allies into expensive wars and weakening their liberal norms as they chose widespread use of emergency powers, surveillance, indefinite detention, torture, and rendition. The war also brought suspicion of Muslims, hostility toward immigration, civilizational rhetoric, and border militarization from the political margins to the center of Western public discourse. America’s preoccupation with the war on terror did squander its unipolar decade. It wasn’t a coincidence that the rise of China—and the China shock—came a few years after the Great Recession of 2008 and a decade after the 9/11 attacks. The cultural and political landscape of Europe and America today—characterized by polarization, populism, and a pervasive distrust of institutions—can be understood, at least in part, as the legacy of the response to the 9/11 attacks by Al-Qaeda. By this measure, my assertion would be that bin Laden won the war on terror much as he had predicted he would. The rise of populism and far-right politics in America and Europe has generated great laments for the corrosion of a golden age of liberalism and progress following the victory of the Allied powers against fascism in World War II. The flowering of postwar liberal democracy owed less to the defeat of fascism than to the Cold War, when the existence of a socialist alternative compelled Europe and North America to emphasize social protections, greater equality, and meaningful political rights. Thus, the crisis of liberal democracy after 9/11 might be better understood not simply as a blowback from the war on terror, but as an aftershock of socialism’s collapse. After the collapse of the Soviet bloc, that pressure disappeared. Though liberal institutions survived formally, the social and economic settlement that sustained them had begun to erode. During World War II and in its immediate aftermath, the Allies were hardly exemplars of liberal standards. Several allied powers were colonial empires engaged in the violent suppression of nationalist movements in Asia and Africa even as they fought fascism. Nor can any account of Allied conduct ignore the firebombing of German cities or the atomic destruction of Hiroshima and Nagasaki. Although the war in Europe and the Pacific dominates debates about Allied misconduct, the colonial character of World War II may tell us more about the origins of our present moment. Colonial armies fought in every theater and constituted some of the forces deployed during the war. At least a million soldiers fighting in the British and French armies against fascism were Muslims. Civilians in territories like India, though far from the principal battlefields, also suffered immensely during the war. The Bengal famine, in which some three million people, Hindus and Muslims, starved to death, was, as many have argued, caused in part by wartime disruptions to grain supplies and by imperial policy. Europe didn’t only take men and material from the colonies for the Great War, but also a repertoire of its illiberal practices in the colonies. The Nazis drew upon precedents established in colonial wars waged by European powers. The Allies, in turn, brought home methods of coercion that had previously been largely confined to their empires. Among them was the revival of medieval doctrines of just war, which subordinated contractual obligations to unilateral claims of moral authority—doctrines that resurfaced during the war on terror to justify pre-emptive attacks and torture. World War II should be understood not simply as liberalism’s triumph over tyranny but also as a source—and continuation—of Western illiberalism, much like what came to the surface after 9/11. It was the Cold War that provided the real foundation of mid-century liberalism. One might even argue that the post-9/11order, from which today’s far-right movements partly emerged, has its roots in World War II’s struggle for global hegemony, a contest suspended for the duration of the Cold War. The war on terror represented a failed effort to impose a unipolar order on the world, one that could no longer rely on the economic instrumentality of globalization alone. And its failure also discredited the globalization associated with free trade and mass migration. In its place arose far-right movements in Western Europe and North America dedicated to dividing the world into racial and cultural blocs, divisions often defended in the language of the struggle against terrorism and Islam. In America, this political vision harks back to the 19th-century Monroe Doctrine; in Europe, it reaches further, to the myth of a culturally homogeneous Christian continent. In both cases, the animating force is not social conservatism alone but hostility toward immigrants, and toward Muslims in particular. Its deeper history may therefore lie in decolonization, which transformed immigration into a defining political reality distinct from the colonial systems of slavery, indenture and managed labor migration that preceded it. Asian and African decolonization not only overlapped with the Cold War but was, in many important ways, made possible by it. As the rival superpowers courted newly independent countries, they helped secure what sovereignty many of those states possessed. Yet decolonization, no less than the Cold War, also made possible the European Economic Community and, eventually, the European Union. Europe emerged from World War II with most of its colonial possessions more or less intact. It was only decolonization that allowed the continent to cohere into a new kind of economic and political unit. Immigration has a double history in America, proceeding from two waves of decolonization: the 19th century independence of Latin American countries and that of Asian and African nations in the 20th century. American anti-immigrant politics consequently differs from the European version, remaining more closely bound to the Monroe Doctrine and its vision of hemispheric control. Europe may possess the older political history, but its encounter with immigration is more recent, starting with labor migration to help with European reconstruction after World War II and expanding again in the wake of the Cold War. The end of the Cold War weakened the sovereignty of many postcolonial states, producing new waves of migration as those countries faced economic pressure or were drawn into conflict. It also reduced European countries from strategic partners in the Cold War to increasingly dependent allies—a relationship laid bare by the war on terror, which expanded American power while disproportionately rewarding American companies. The end of the Cold War, made visible through 9/11 and its aftermath, has also transformed Europe’s understanding of its past. While World War II is still invoked, especially by liberals, it no longer occupies its former place as the 20th century’s defining event and the founding moment of the international order now coming apart. The ascendance of immigration—and, with it, race and culture—as the central political concern on both sides of the Atlantic suggests that decolonization has displaced the war in the historical imagination. Narratives about the struggles against fascism or Communism still shape Western identities, but political debate is increasingly dominated by fears of what might be called “reverse colonization.” Europe’s unwillingness or inability to reckon with colonialism may explain its strange, even pathological identification with the anxieties of its former subjects. The fear of foreign settlement, demographic change, and cultural swamping all echo the language once used in colonized societies of Asia and Africa, where the Christian West was regarded as precisely such a threat. This switching of places is apparent in portrayals of Islam as both an alien and more virile force than Western liberalism, a force whose supposed cohesion and will to dominate must be countered by weapons of solidarity and domination. European politics has thus increasingly begun to appropriate the defensive posture of the peoples Europe once ruled. Perverse though this identification may be, it reveals the extent to which decolonization—itself part of the Cold War’s history— has become the century’s central historical drama. This switching of places is especially true of Islam seen as an alien but also more virile force than Western liberalism, one whose power thus needs to be countered by its own weapons of solidarity and domination. For all the spectacle of its asymmetric attacks, Al Qaeda—and Islamist militancy more broadly—never had more than some tens of thousands of committed supporters worldwide. These movements did not emerge from some hermetically sealed Islamic history. They belonged to global events and narratives, which gave them what influence they possessed. The disproportion between their small numbers and enormous consequences cannot be explained primarily by technology, mass media or even the war on terror. It arose from the institutional and political vulnerabilities exposed by the Cold War’s belated conclusion. We now live amid the detritus of the end of the Cold War and are able to recognize decolonization as the crucial event of the 20th century. Its legacy animates both movements seeking greater inclusion and campaigns promising to restore a national unity defined in racial or cultural terms. More than three decades after the Soviet Union’s collapse, we still live in the wake of the Cold War, an aftermath that first revealed its force in the attacks of Sept. 11, 2001 on New York and Washington, D.C.

Yemen’s Houthis Capture Red Sea Port City, Threatening New Front in War

Yemen’s Houthis Capture Red Sea Port City, Threatening New Front in War

TIME | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings People sit in a fishing boat sailing the Bab el-Mandeb Strait off of Yemen's southern Taiz province on July 25, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Iran-aligned Houthi rebels have captured Yemen’s port city of Mokha from Saudi-backed government forces, potentially opening up a second theater in the war between the U.S. and Iran. The capture of Mokha marks the Houthis’ largest territorial gain since a 2022 cease-fire in Yemen that paused major fighting between the group and Yemen’s government. The Houthis also disrupted shipping in 2024 in response to Israel’s occupation and bombardment of Gaza, which prompted the U.S. and U.K. to carry out deadly strikes on Yemen. The Houthis swept south along the Red Sea coast before taking Mokha on Thursday and subsequently reached the Hanish archipelago, Yemeni government sources told Reuters. A Yemeni commander and a member of the Houthis’ political bureau separately confirmed the Houthi capture of Mokha to the Associated Press. Government forces retreated south toward Dhubab, which overlooks Bab el-Mandeb. Yemen’s government has reportedly launched airstrikes west of Taiz and warned civilians to avoid roads between Taiz and Mokha. Houthi-owned Al-Masirah TV reported that Saudi-backed forces launched 40 airstrikes on the provinces of Taiz, Jawf, Hodeida, and Marib. Observers and Yemeni residents fear the escalation could renew an all-out civil war in Yemen. “This renewed war should alarm us all,” Hans Grundberg, the U.N. special envoy for Yemen, said at an emergency meeting of the U.N. Security Council on Thursday. “It effectively marks the end of the relative calm that Yemen has experienced for over four years since the U.N.-brokered truce.” Capturing the southwestern port also strengthens the Houthis’ ability to threaten shipping through Bab el-Mandeb, a waterway through which around 12% of global trade normally passes. The strait, which lies between Yemen, Djibouti and Eritrea and links the Red Sea with the Gulf of Aden, has become more important since Iran militarized the Strait of Hormuz in retaliation for the U.S. and Israel’s Feb. 28 strikes. The Houthis reportedly received direct guidance and weapons from Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC). Iran reportedly told the Houthis last week to escalate their attacks on Saudi Arabia. Iran publicly maintains that the Houthis are “not a proxy.” The country’s foreign ministry said in a statement on Friday that the conflict in Yemen must be resolved through dialogue instead of military action. It also invoked what it called the “expansionism of the Israeli regime with U.S. complicity.” Mohammed Abdulsalam, a spokesperson for the Houthis, posted on social media that the group will stop targeting Saudi ships when the “aggression” against Yemen ends. A Houthi-run body in Yemen that communicates with commercial shipping companies said navigation through Bab el-Mandeb remained safe, except for Saudi vessels. The advance comes as Tehran has sought new ways to counter Washington’s economic pressure campaign and military attacks. A stronger Houthi position along the Red Sea coast could further constrain global energy supplies, raise shipping and insurance costs, and push up oil prices. Even if the Houthis are acting in their own interest, Iran could gain significant leverage over Washington in conjunction with continued pressure on traffic through the Strait of Hormuz. The capture of Mokha came hours after President Donald Trump on Wednesday suggested that Iran’s government was prolonging the war in an attempt to affect Republican prospects in the U.S. midterm elections in November. He nevertheless insisted Iran is on the verge of collapse and that the war will end “immediately after” the vote. From Mokha, which lies around 80 km north of Bab el-Mandeb, the Houthis have a stronger position to disrupt shipping, particularly targeting vessels travelling to and from Saudi Arabia, which is the world’s biggest crude exporter and a close U.S. ally. Saudi Arabia has routed more oil to its Red Sea port of Yanbu after the effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz. But the Houthis declared a maritime blockade of Saudi Arabia on July 20 and began attacking Saudi shipping and energy infrastructure. Saudi crude exports have fallen to their lowest level in 13 years, with production dropping 23% from July to August. Overall vessel traffic through Bab el-Mandeb fell around 24% in the week following the July blockade. The kingdom has responded with airstrikes in Yemen’s north and warned Iran to rein in the group. It has supplied its Yemeni allies with arms, intelligence, and logistical assistance. The Saudi government is reportedly reticent to escalate its fight with the Houthis and has so far resisted requests for a broader air campaign in the south of Yemen and along the Red Sea coast. The advance could also force the U.S. to devote military resources towards protecting a second maritime chokepoint and assisting its ally, potentially complicating efforts to limit or swiftly end U.S. involvement in the Middle East.

Trump Administration Proposes Cutting Grace Period for H-1B and Other Visas

Update your preferences in Account Settings A pedestrian walks past an exterior sign and official seal identifying the U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS) field office building in Salt Lake City, Utah, on Aug. 21, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The Department of Homeland Security has proposed scrapping the policy allowing certain foreign workers—including H-1B visa holders—to remain in the U.S. for up to 60 days after losing their jobs to find a new sponsor, according to a government notice published Friday. The White House-approved proposal is the latest disruption to the H-1B visa, which sits at the intersection of the Trump Administration’s priorities to restrict legal immigration, put an “America First” policy to work in the labor sector, and increase oversight of tech companies that rely on workers under this category. Under the new proposal, some foreign workers would be considered immediately removable from the U.S. if they no longer work for an employer linked to their visa status. The proposal “restores a direct relationship between an alien’s nonimmigrant status and the specific employment or activity that formed the basis of his or her admission or grant of status in the United States and reduces administrative burden,” according to the notice in the Federal Register. Public comments on the proposal are accepted for 60 days. If adopted, the policy would mark one of the biggest changes to immigration policy, almost a decade after the grace period was created. In early 2017, during the final days of the Obama Administration, a rule took effect stating that foreign citizens with legal immigration status under the H-1B visa, as well as several other visa categories, would have a 60-day grace period after they lose their job. That leeway allowed these workers to look for a new job or to change their immigration status. The other visas covered by the grace period are E-1 treaty trader visas and E-2 treaty investor visas; L-1 visas allowing short-term work for executives and managers with international firms; O-1 ⁠visas for ​individuals with “extraordinary ability” in science, sports, or the arts; and TN visas for qualified Canadian and Mexican professionals. The grace period also covers H-1B1 visas for nationals of Chile or Singapore working in a specialty occupation, and E-3 visas for Australian citizens working in a specialized professional occupation. According to DHS’s calculations, nearly 4,000 workers a year utilize the grace period to submit a new nonimmigrant worker petition after a layoff or resignation. More than 99% of them are H-1B visa holders. Eliminating the grace period would favor American laborers, the DHS argued. “DHS assumes that almost all these entities would offer the same jobs to equally qualified U.S. workers, go through the I-129 petition process to sponsor nonimmigrant workers, or reassign the work to other current employees,” the document read. Its removal would also reduce administrative work, as the existing rule states the DHS could skip or shorten the grace period at its discretion. According to the document, from Oct. 1, 2017, through May 20, 2026, the DHS calculated 1.9 million petitions or applications on which USCIS had to assess whether the 60-day period could have potentially applied. The DHS acknowledges that the policy changes would not only affect prospective employers but also families of the workers who may be forced to leave the U.S. The proposal could also affect the immigration status of dependents of H-1B visa holders. Immigration advocacy group FWD.us estimates about 730,000 H-1B visa holders living in the U.S., plus 550,000 dependents, including spouses and children. But according to the proposal, the department says it believes “the harm of the up to 60-day discretionary grace period outweighs the potential benefit it provides to the impacted aliens and employers, the alien’s dependents, and the community at large.” Since 1990, hundreds of thousands of skilled foreign workers have entered the U.S. through the H-1B visa system. Citizens from India make up the bulk of H-1B visa holders, according to an April U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services report, with China a distant second. Tech industry titans like Microsoft’s Satya Nadella, Google’s Sundar Pichai, and even SpaceX’s Elon Musk have stayed in the U.S. with the help of H-1B visas. Congress has set the current annual cap for new H-1B visas at 65,000, though an additional 20,000 is allotted for those with advanced degrees. Demand for H-1B visas often outpaces the cap. But the Trump Administration has subjected the H-1B visa to various changes, as Trump claims skilled foreign workers have taken away jobs from Americans. It has since replaced the traditional H-1B lottery with a weighted system favoring higher-skilled, higher-paid workers. And while a court blocked an attempt to impose a $100,000 fee on certain H-1B employers, the Administration in August proposed a new $103,265 fee for certain H-1B petitions and expanded a $4,000 biometric and security fee to cover extension applications from employers with a large foreign workforce.
The Atlantic
Rules Designed to Create Chaos
2026-09-11

Rules Designed to Create Chaos

The Atlantic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Donald Trump hates mail-in voting. He has described it as “dangerous,” “corrupt,” “horrible,” and “a fraud.” Last year, in a Truth Social post festooned with capital letters and exclamation points, he announced that he would be signing an executive order to do away with mail-in ballots entirely. That order never materialized, possibly because the president has absolutely no legal authority to carry out such a ban. The federal government plays only a small role in administering elections, and the Constitution locates those authorities with Congress, not the president. But now, with some ballots already heading to voters as the midterms draw near, the Trump administration is waging another fight against voting by mail, this time through its control over the U.S. Postal Service. Trump’s plan wouldn’t outright prohibit mail-in voting but would throw so many wrenches into the machinery that it could grind to a halt. Federal courts at both the district and appellate levels have blocked the rule, and now the Supreme Court is in the process of weighing whether to allow this effort to move forward before the midterms; it could make a decision as soon as later today. If the justices let USPS take these steps in the coming weeks, the risk, the election-law expert Rick Hasen told me, is “mass disenfranchisement.” Historically, the Postal Service has simply mailed every ballot provided by state and local election officials. But in a March executive order, Trump directed USPS to develop a new plan that would erect a series of hoops for election workers to jump through before a ballot can be sent to an expectant voter. If the rule goes into effect, USPS will require state and local jurisdictions to obtain federal sign-off on the design of the envelope and then mark each ballot envelope with barcodes. States must also upload a full list of every voter receiving a mail ballot to a so-called federal ballot mail portal. Only then, once USPS cross-checks the barcodes with the voter list in the portal, will the ballot be delivered. From the October 2026 issue: The election deniers’ long game In the abstract, this might sound inoffensive. But the process is so hopelessly tangled that it seems almost designed to fail. Take the portal, which is still not online. On September 3—a week ago—the Postal Service stated in a court filing that the website would be ready “by sometime next week.” Even if the portal were functional, it might not operate fairly. A whistleblower recently filed a disclosure warning that the Postal Service is planning to use the system with a “zero percent failure rate.” USPS will scan ballots in large batches, the whistleblower says, and if a single ballot fails to match the uploaded list of voters, none of the ballots in the batch will be mailed. Election rules take a long time to change, and states begin preparing the polls long before Election Day. When Trump announced the order, in March, experts did not immediately think that the midterms would be affected. “I had assumed that this was going to be put on hold” until after November, Hasen said. But USPS quickly began putting together the detailed plans requested by the White House, even though the order itself faced multiple legal challenges and was racking up court judgments against it. (Another section of the executive order directed the Department of Homeland Security to produce a mechanism by which states can review “citizenship lists” of potential voters. DHS has yet to provide these lists, and the nonprofit Electronic Privacy Information Center recently sued to block the agency from compiling and distributing them.) The Supreme Court ruled on August 24 that the executive order’s challengers had gone to court too soon, before USPS’s plans were far enough along. Now the Postal Service claims that its work is ready—mostly—to be rolled out, if the justices will let it. The court dockets are crammed with statements by election experts and state and local officials explaining all of the different problems created by the rule. In their breadth and variety, these declarations are a reminder of just how big the United States is, and how many people must work together across multiple states and levels of government to pull off a successful election season. Hawaii has spent more than $79,000 printing ballot envelopes, the state’s chief election officer wrote in one filing; he worried that if officials need to order new envelopes, they may not arrive from the continental U.S. in time for the election. Rhode Island’s deputy secretary of state explained that the state’s laws regulating ballot-envelope design effectively prohibit officials from affixing the two barcodes required by USPS. In Wisconsin, some rural jurisdictions may not have the ability to print the required barcodes, the chair of the Wisconsin Elections Commission said. The elections director in Oregon, which conducts all of the state’s elections completely by mail, worried that the state’s “aging” computer systems are not equipped to efficiently upload 3 million voters’ personal information into the USPS portal. “If the system fails,” she wrote, “Oregon may be unable to mail ballots to voters at all.” Election officials who have long relied on the Postal Service are now thinking through alternatives. Cisco Aguilar, Nevada’s secretary of state, told me that he has reached out to private mail carriers about delivering ballots in a pinch. “We got a quote from UPS of about eight bucks per ballot, which is about three dollars more than we pay the Postal Service,” he said. “With 2.1 million ballots in Nevada, that’s a pretty significant expense.” Nevada sends mail-in ballots to voters by default, and mail-in voting is popular: 60 percent of Nevadans voted by mail in the state’s primary elections, Aguilar said. Now his office is figuring out how to communicate clearly with voters who may be alarmed or confused if USPS refuses to mail Nevada’s ballots. “Do we just start to message, Hey, just skip the mail ballot altogether and show up in person?” Experts are hoping that the Supreme Court takes these concerns seriously. Because of the emergency status of the litigation, the justices can consider not just the legality of the federal government’s actions but also the “equities”—that is, the potential harms suffered by each side depending on how the Court rules. There are strong legal arguments that the administration has no authority to put such a program in place. (Perhaps tellingly, the federal government has been reduced to arguing that the Post Office can impose requirements for mailing ballots in the same way that it can put safeguards in place for mailing cremated human remains.) On the equities, though, the matter seems particularly stark. If the Supreme Court allows the policy to go into effect, the outcome could be catastrophic. But if the policy remains blocked, what is the harm to the federal government? The administration’s briefs make little attempt to answer this question other than vague insinuations about voter fraud. Then again, a victory at the Court wouldn’t appear to gain much for the administration, either. If the goal is partisan sabotage, there is little reason to think that the USPS plan would help Republicans. “If this actually happens, the burden is going to fall hardest on the most vulnerable of our voters—the elderly, the disabled, rural voters, voters on reservations, voters in the military,” Hasen said. Older voters and rural Americans have tended to be reliable GOP constituencies, and in a season when the Republican Party is struggling to build up voter enthusiasm, there is a real risk that inconvenienced voters who never receive a ballot will simply stay home. Democrats furious with Trump, meanwhile, may be more motivated to find a way to the polls. Still, the widespread confusion that the USPS rule would inevitably create may have its own appeal to the White House. A group of election officials, including several Republican secretaries of state, warned in an amicus brief before the Supreme Court that “a chaotic election erodes confidence in the result.” For a president long obsessed with undermining Americans’ trust in elections, perhaps that’s victory enough.

The Atlantic
America Has Forfeited Its Greatest Strength
2026-09-11

America Has Forfeited Its Greatest Strength

The Atlantic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Perhaps the most famous conclusion of the 9/11 Commission Report is that America’s inability to prevent the September 11 attacks resulted from a “failure of imagination.” With respect to the 9/11 Commission, that is not quite right. Long before September 11, members of the U.S. intelligence community understood the awful scale of Osama bin Laden’s ambitions. The presidential daily brief on August 6, 2001, included an item titled “Bin Ladin Determined to Strike in US,” which even referenced al-Qaeda’s desire to hijack an American aircraft. This was just one of 36 articles in presidential briefings that year that mentioned bin Laden or al-Qaeda. As an FBI special agent, I participated in investigations of al-Qaeda both before and after 9/11. Based on my experience, the main reason the U.S. didn’t stop 9/11 was not a failure of imagination but a failure of trust. America’s intelligence institutions lacked confidence in one another. In fact, they hid intelligence from their peers. This particular problem has improved since the attacks, but new, even graver, failures of trust have emerged. As 9/11 and its aftermath have shown, American security depends on many kinds of faith. Allies must trust U.S. leadership. Citizens must have confidence in their government. Policy makers must believe in America’s deepest norms and values. Over the past 25 years, the U.S. has frayed each of these bonds of trust. This is exactly what bin Laden sought to achieve. He often referred to a text called “Management of Savagery,” dubbed the “al-Qaeda manual,” which recalled the collapse of the Roman empire in the face of sustained pressure from small frontier tribes. The jihadist mission was to subject the U.S. to the same fate—turning its allies into enemies by eradicating the world’s trust in American power and ideals. The 9/11 attacks alone did not accomplish that goal. But 25 years later, America’s own mistakes have brought it closer to fulfillment than bin Laden ever could. Several weeks before 9/11, the FBI sent me to Sanaa, Yemen, to join an interagency team investigating al-Qaeda’s murder of 17 U.S. sailors. Al-Qaeda had bombed USS Cole in October 2000 as the ship lay at anchor in a Yemeni port. We soon learned the first of a series of details about the attack that have played in my mind ever since. In January 2000, when planning for the bombing was well under way, two al-Qaeda operatives left Yemen for Thailand with $36,000 in cash. Both would participate in the eventual attack. A few days later, a third, more senior al-Qaeda operative known as Khallad flew from Malaysia to Thailand and collected the money. Hillary Rodham Clinton: What the aftermath of 9/11 taught me We knew that al-Qaeda had been plotting to attack a U.S. warship in Yemen. So why would the group move two operatives and a large amount of cash out of the country? We sought answers from our partners across the U.S. intelligence community. Each time, the response was the same: No one knew anything that could help our investigation. I was still in Sanaa on September 11. From thousands of miles away, I saw images of smoke billowing from the Pentagon, twin fireballs bringing down the World Trade Center, and wreckage lying strewn across a field in my home state of Pennsylvania. Along with the rest of the Cole team, I was ordered to evacuate Yemen. The next day, when I was already at the airport, I got a call: Someone had information to share about the attacks. Instead of boarding my flight, I raced to the U.S. embassy, where a CIA officer handed me a file with the answers my team had been seeking. The $36,000 hadn’t been meant for Khallad, as we’d thought. Apparently it had paid for two other al-Qaeda members, Khalid al-Mihdhar and Nawaf al-Hazmi, to travel to the United States. The purpose of their trip had just been revealed: Al-Mihdhar and al-Hazmi were two of the hijackers who crashed American Airlines Flight 77 into the Pentagon. The pair had been in the United States for months, and had left a significant paper trail. As both the CIA inspector general and the 9/11 Commission would conclude years later, the two hijackers could have been tracked down and arrested if this information had been shared with the Cole team. The “planes operation,” as al-Qaeda called it, might have been stopped. All at once, I realized that we could have saved thousands of lives if only we’d had the relevant intelligence. I have yet to overcome the horror of that discovery. I suspect I never will. After the attacks, the intelligence community and law enforcement worked together on a sweeping investigation to track down those responsible and prepare the government to retaliate. In Yemen, a colleague and I questioned Abu Jandal, a suspect in the Cole investigation, gleaning crucial information from him about al-Qaeda and bin Laden. In keeping with traditional American interrogation methods, we pretended that we were his friends and made him believe that we already knew the information we needed. These techniques worked, as they usually did. Jandal identified several of the 9/11 hijackers as al-Qaeda members. This information allowed President George W. Bush to inform the American people that bin Laden was responsible for the attacks. It was also instrumental in persuading several leaders in the Muslim world, most notably Pervez Musharraf of Pakistan, to acquiesce to the invasion of Afghanistan. The 9/11 investigation progressed in parallel with military action against al-Qaeda and its Taliban enablers. Interagency cooperation improved. More suspects were apprehended. At that moment, it was still possible to believe that America’s response to the attacks would be sober, measured, and led by sound intelligence. Within a few months, however, I would be disabused of that notion. By the spring of 2002, I had joined another interagency team and began interrogating Abu Zubaydah, a fixer who often worked with al-Qaeda, though he wasn’t a member. Almost as soon as we sat down, he identified himself as “Daood.” I smiled. We had caught him in a lie. “How about I call you Hani?” I said, using his childhood nickname. Zubaydah’s jaw dropped. He seemed to realize that we would know if he continued trying to deceive us, so he started cooperating. Within an hour, Zubaydah had divulged the details of a developing terrorist plot. We reported the information to Washington, and the attack was foiled. Zubaydah proceeded to tell us everything he knew about the “planes operation,” including the identity of its mastermind, Khalid Sheikh Mohammed. After about 10 productive days, a team from the CIA’s Counterterrorism Mission Center arrived alongside an external contractor, who claimed that he could get even more information out of Zubaydah. He described a series of techniques that were designed to establish the interrogator in Zubaydah’s mind as “God.” The contractor had Zubaydah stripped naked, pumped loud music into his cell, and deprived him of sleep. These were part of a set of methods that would come to be known euphemistically as “enhanced interrogation techniques.” It is important to recognize that these techniques did not reflect the CIA’s usual practice, or the painstaking work of most of its veteran officers. The agency’s traditional interrogation program, which combined lawful questioning with the exploitation of well-documented evidence, achieved great successes when it was not compromised by ineffective torture methods. Indeed, many of the 9/11 investigation’s early breakthroughs, including with Zubaydah, were achieved with the help of CIA officers. Their valuable work must not be confused with the torture program, which was largely designed and implemented by contractors. Subjected to the new, unproven techniques, Zubaydah immediately clammed up. This was unsurprising: Most jihadist terrorists are trained to withstand even harsher methods, including ones involving physical and sexual violence. Eventually, the contractor was pulled back and the interagency team was allowed to interrogate Zubaydah once more. He started talking again, identifying additional al-Qaeda members and associates. Still, the promoters of the new techniques were not satisfied. They insisted that Zubaydah was a top al-Qaeda official rather than an outside fixer, and turned the temperature in his cell as low as it would go. Once again Zubaydah stopped talking, until the interagency team was called back in. This back-and-forth continued until the contractor’s methods became so extreme and grotesque—one involved a custom-built coffin in which he planned to imprison Zubaydah—that FBI headquarters became unwilling to be associated with the techniques. The torture program not only breached American norms and values; it yielded fake intelligence. Among other falsehoods, Zubaydah “confessed” under torture to being the third-ranking al-Qaeda official. He later told more conventional interrogators that he had said that only to make the torture stop. These methods introduced violence into our intelligence gathering and ignored decades of accumulated wisdom about effective interrogation. They also alienated our allies. The Senate Select Committee on Intelligence found that the program “caused immeasurable damage to the United States’ public standing, as well as to the United States’ longstanding global leadership on human rights in general and the prevention of torture in particular.” The interrogation program was just one example of a cardinal error that the U.S. government made after 9/11: It abandoned norms and values embodied in the Constitution; America’s commitment to human rights; and more practical guidelines, such as the Army Field Manuals, which enshrine the traditional, highly effective interrogation methods that my colleagues and I used on Jandal and Zubaydah. These were replaced with untested, ad hoc experiments that appeared to prioritize seeking vengeance over protecting American lives. The CIA inspector general did not identify a single imminent plot that the enhanced interrogation techniques had actually thwarted. The program was finally outlawed by Congress in 2015, but it continues to undermine American security. Torture produced faulty intelligence that has shaped policy decisions whose effects still linger. Most notable, it contributed to arguably the United States’ worst misstep since 9/11: the decision to go to war in Iraq. In the months following the attacks, many counterterrorism professionals came under pressure to find a link between al-Qaeda and Sadam Hussein’s government in Iraq. I was one of them. In addition to the intelligence and counterterrorism work I was doing during the day, I returned to my quarters every evening to a barrage of emails seeking proof of a connection that did not exist. The enhanced interrogation techniques played an important role in the search for the longed-for link. The same contractor who had compromised Abu Zubaydah’s interrogation participated in the interrogation of Zubaydah’s partner, the al-Qaeda operative Ibn al-Shaykh al-Libi. I wasn’t involved in al-Libi’s interrogation, but, like Zubaydah, he had reportedly been cooperating under the traditional approach. After the contractor arrived, al-Libi was subjected to torture in a third country. As al-Libi later freely confessed, he “decided he would fabricate any information the interrogators wanted in order to gain better treatment and avoid being handed over” to a foreign government. One such fabrication was the purported link between al-Qaeda and the Iraqi regime. This became a key piece of evidence used by policy makers seeking to justify war in Iraq. America’s leaders began the war in Afghanistan with clear mandates, limited goals, and broad coalitions. The 2003 invasion of Iraq, by contrast, featured vague, ambitious aims and took place over the objections of many U.S. allies. It looked more like a war of vendetta—or, worse, one of empire building. The Senate Select Committee on Intelligence found that George W. Bush’s administration had “repeatedly presented intelligence as fact when in reality it was unsubstantiated, contradicted, or even non-existent.” The consequences have been devastating. When it became clear that the Iraq War had begun under false pretenses, American citizens’ faith in their government took a nosedive, and allies’ confidence was shaken. Moreover, the conflict played directly into al-Qaeda’s core narrative: the false idea that the West and its henchmen were waging an epochal war against Islam. The invasion of Iraq, the torture program, the humiliating conditions at Guantánamo Bay and prisons such as Abu Ghraib, America’s apparent indifference to civilian casualties from air strikes—each became a powerful recruitment tool for terrorists. As of September 11, 2001, al-Qaeda likely had only a few hundred members, most of them based in Afghanistan. Today, the U.S. intelligence community estimates that the group has 15,000 to 28,000 members worldwide. Finally, by splitting its attention and resources across two wars, the United States doomed its fortunes in both. In 2011, the withdrawal from Iraq after eight grueling years paved the way for the Islamic State to take over much of the country. At the same time, U.S. objectives in Afghanistan crept further from the limited goals articulated in 2001. It became U.S. policy to attempt to shape Afghanistan into a Western-style, centralized republic, whether the Afghan people wanted that or not. Pursuing that doomed, messianic mission for 20 years ultimately cost the United States about $2.3 trillion; 2,219 American military members’ lives; and a great deal of trust around the world. The eventual withdrawal from Afghanistan, shortly before the 20th anniversary of 9/11, seemed stage-managed to show just how steeply American power had declined over those two decades. The images of desperate Afghans clinging to departing aircraft became defining symbols of the end of America’s longest war. Much like the fall of Saigon and the evacuation of the U.S. embassy there in 1975, those scenes will continue to taint the world’s perceptions of American power, credibility, and commitment. Even worse, the U.S. is repeating the same mistakes today. In February, the United States began its war against Iran without broad support from its allies in the region. Soon the conflict unleashed significant political, military, and economic damage on many of those allies, prompting some of them to question the value of U.S. security guarantees. Some American allies, previously content to rely on the U.S. security umbrella, have begun making alternative arrangements. In August, Turkey, a NATO member, entered into a mutual-defense pact with Saudi Arabia and Pakistan. Hakan Fidan, Turkey’s foreign minister, didn’t hide his country’s reasoning: “External hegemons coming into the region do not solve the problems; they make them more acute, and the cost becomes very high.” America’s relationships with the Muslim world have suffered in other ways too. In March, as the threat of Iranian drone and missile attacks escalated, the State Department ordered nonemergency embassy personnel to leave Qatar, Bahrain, Jordan, Oman, Saudi Arabia, and the United Arab Emirates. Meanwhile, operations at embassies in Iraq and Lebanon have been hampered for some time by threats and attacks from Iranian-backed militants. These disruptions have compounded a bigger problem: The U.S. has far fewer active embassies in the region today than it did before 9/11. This will have significant long-term consequences. When diplomatic channels disappear, cooperation between militaries and law enforcement erodes, as do bilateral trade and investment. Intelligence agencies lose human eyes and ears on the ground. Peaceful relations become harder to maintain. Miscalculation grows likelier. Even as it disengages from important relationships in the Middle East, the United States has introduced confusion into alliances elsewhere. In recent years, American policy makers have given Taiwan mixed messages, oscillating from staunch support to requests for the country to shoulder a greater security burden. The U.S. has called into question its formal security guarantee with Japan and, in the opening weeks of the Iran war, redeployed some 2,500 Marines that had previously been stationed there. Around the same time, Washington reportedly diverted missile-defense interceptors from South Korea to the Middle East. All of these moves threaten to diminish U.S. power in the Indo-Pacific at a time when Chinese influence is growing. In Europe, the Iran conflict may have distracted attention and resources from the war in Ukraine. According to the Associated Press, a “sizable number” of Patriot missiles had been moved out of Europe, bound for the Middle East. Peace negotiations stalled, reportedly because U.S. attention suddenly pivoted toward Iran. Thanks to spiking energy prices, Russian oil revenues and tax receipts surged, leading one prominent think tank to characterize the war in Iran as “an economic gift for Putin.” The conflict has also coincided with a decline in trust among America’s hitherto staunchest allies. In May, polling by the European Council on Foreign Relations found that only 11 percent of respondents across 15 European democracies still considered the United States an ally. As Europe and Asia drift further from America’s orbit, the U.S.-led international order comes closer to collapse, just as Osama bin Laden dreamed. America’s own citizens are scarcely less skeptical than those of its allies. At the outset of hostilities with Iran, 62 percent said they had not been given a clear explanation of the reasons for war. It is not difficult to see why. As recently as June 2025, senior administration officials had claimed that the United States had “destroyed the Iranian nuclear program.” Less than nine months later, the same officials were citing the continued existence of that program as a casus belli. The shifting official justifications have resembled the obfuscation and outright lies that underpinned America’s invasion of Iraq. But whereas the Iraq War was popular at the outset—about three-quarters of Americans favored military action—a majority of U.S. voters opposed the Iran conflict as soon as it started. Americans have many reasons for opposing the war. But the profound skepticism with which so many U.S. citizens regard their government has clear roots in 9/11. The distrust has only grown since the attacks because of a systematic lack of accountability. Families who lost spouses, partners, parents, and children in the 9/11 attacks have spent 25 years waiting for justice. Some have died before seeing those responsible enter a courtroom. Read: The anniversary I wish I didn’t share with my husband The U.S. government has denied accountability not only for the attackers but also for American officials who made significant mistakes. To use a personal example: The CIA inspector general recommended that an accountability board review the performance of the officials who failed to hand over information my team had asked for in Yemen. This recommendation was summarily rejected by the CIA director. As recently as 2024, prosecutors reached plea deals with Khalid Sheikh Mohammed and two other suspects, partly designed to induce them to give depositions in exchange for lifelong imprisonment rather than the death penalty. Yet the Department of Defense revoked those plea deals just two days later. Not all of the victims’ families supported them, but the deals would not have been necessary in the first place if the U.S. government had simply followed its own laws while interrogating suspects, rather than compromising evidence by obtaining it through torture. Still, the families continue to fight, including Terry Strada, whose husband was killed in the World Trade Center. “The question is whether the United States will stand with the victims of terrorism or with those who financed and enabled it,” Strada wrote in 2024. “Our grief has not faded. Neither has our determination. We will not accept selective memory or performative sympathy, and we will not be silent, because without accountability our national security is weakened, and our country is more vulnerable to a recurrence of past threats.” In the years since 9/11, a lack of accountability has become endemic across the U.S. government. The enhanced-interrogation-techniques program is another example: Not a single official has been prosecuted, not even those who were connected to the two detainees who died in U.S. custody. Instead, the contractors who devised the program received indemnity and $81 million for their services. As the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence found, some agencies of the U.S. government obscured or misrepresented the truth about the techniques not only to the CIA inspector general but to Congress, the Department of Justice, and even the White House. It appears that nobody has been held accountable. At the end of 1974, in the midst of the Vietnam War and the fallout from Watergate, the proportion of Americans who trusted their government to do the right thing “just about always” or “most of the time” fell to what was then an unprecedented low of 36 percent. Faith in government later rebounded somewhat. On the eve of 9/11, it stood at 44 percent, then peaked at 55 percent as Americans rallied around their government in the month after the attacks. But by 2025, this trust had declined to 17 percent—less than half of its post-Watergate nadir. Such low trust in the state degrades the social contract. It reduces support for everything the government does, including—and perhaps especially—national security. Faced with a redacted page or a whitewashed report, people will choose something else to believe in—in many cases something considerably darker than the truth. Conspiracy theories, amplified by social media, further erode the public’s confidence. September 11, 2001, is a day that never really ended. Its consequences continue to be felt whenever America is tempted to risk the little trust that it still enjoys, whether by confusing vengeance with security, impunity with strength, or callousness with patriotism. The only way for the United States to rebuild its global leadership is by recommitting to the values our enemies hoped to make us abandon: truth, accountability, law, decency, and the protection of innocent life. Only then will we have escaped the long shadow of 9/11.
The Atlantic
How to Kill a Franchise Before It Even Starts
2026-09-11

How to Kill a Franchise Before It Even Starts

The Atlantic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
The marketing for Hope, the new film by the South Korean horror master Na Hong-jin, presents it on an epic, otherworldly scale. It’s the most expensive Korean movie ever made, and the director’s first in more than 10 years, since his widely acclaimed hit, The Wailing; its sweeping rollout across the United States, which includes special showings on premium-format screens, is also one rarely afforded to international productions. That approach befits Hope’s run time (a hefty 156 minutes); its blockbuster plot, about an alien invasion taking place near Korea’s Demilitarized Zone; and its open-endedness—apparently, Na has already written a sequel. Yet the entire time I was watching it, I had one overriding feeling: Everything could perhaps be a little bit smaller. Hope’s first hour, after all, does not really hint at these greater ambitions. It comes across as simply an extremely robust monster thriller, mixing action, horror, and comedy with aplomb. The story initially concerns a slightly bumbling police officer, Bum-seok (played by Hwang Jung-min), who is summoned by local hunters to investigate a cow carcass covered in mysterious claw marks. Bum-seok’s fears of an escaped zoo animal escalate as he returns to the town of Hope Harbor to find that carnage has erupted. Na has the camera (and his anxious protagonist) navigate piled-up vehicles and trashed houses with jumpy timidity, building tension beautifully as Bum-seok tries to figure out exactly what could have caused such destruction. Through all of the early action, I was most reminded of solid creature features such as Tremors, Eight Legged Freaks, and Crawl—the kind of delightful Hollywood pablum we don’t get enough of anymore. Entries in this genre tend to pose the question of what would happen if a few oversize monsters trashed a town with impunity. Another obvious influence is Bong Joon-ho’s The Host, which is more of a major-disaster flick; it features a massive tentacled kaiju while also playing on fears of blundering bureaucracy and capitalistic overreach—subjects common in Korean cinema. Hope has similar fun poking at local law enforcement’s shambling initial response to catastrophe: The townspeople (many of them retirement-age) take their defense upon themselves and drive around in pickup trucks holding shotguns. But as with any movie of this ilk, the buildup is often more fun than the reveal, and Hope begins to falter as we see more of the beings terrorizing the land. Recent reports put its budget at nearly $75 million, which is a considerable amount for the South Korean film industry, but is still on the lower end for a project this ambitious. As the action drags on and on, it becomes clear just how grand Na’s vision is; slow-motion gunplay unfolds in sequence after sequence, where the embattled cops shoot at the CGI-rendered creatures besieging them, and the digital imagery can’t quite keep up with the otherwise crisp visuals. I am perfectly willing to overlook some of those limitations—I applaud Na’s big swing—but the director might have benefited from pulling back on the garishly low-resolution monster effects and leaving more to the imagination. Alas, he did not. The audience is instead treated to bipedal invaders drawn from motion-capture performances by Hollywood stars, including Michael Fassbender and Alicia Vikander. The script nods to a wider universe of stories that will establish how the aliens showed up and what their plans are for our planet. But Hope itself explains very little, a somewhat shocking turn of events given its immense length. Around the hundredth time that I watched townspeople fire machine guns at half speed in a striking landscape (much of the film’s second half takes place in a beautiful, forested area with very tall trees), I realized that a broader exposition probably wasn’t forthcoming. Hope doesn’t have much of an ending at all, choosing to nod toward a potential future installment. The decision is frustrating; several of Na’s teases about the movie’s world are intriguing, but a punchless third act could turn off audiences—and thus cost him the opportunity to depict it further. The director’s skill with action filmmaking and the highs of the tensest set pieces should have been enough to excite me for a sequel, if Na does succeed in getting one produced. But after throwing some thrilling opening haymakers, Hope doesn’t offer enough to really encourage me back.
