The Verge
Anime reaction YouTubers are at war with copyright enforcers
2026-09-11

Anime reaction YouTubers are at war with copyright enforcers

The Verge | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Content creators say their videos are fair use, but one controversial company begs to differ.﻿ On September 6th, YouTuber Nicholas Light posted a video to one of his channels that made it seem like all of his content was about to be removed from the platform. In the video — ominously titled “My Channel will be Deleted Today” — Light alleges that he was on the receiving end of some of the “thousands upon thousands of false copyright strikes” recently initiated by a company called Remove Your Media. Light spends much of the 13-minute video accusing Remove Your Media CEO Eric Green of being a troll who systematically targets creators whose content mentions or features clips of popular anime series like Bleach and One Piece. It’s not clear how many of Light’s 1.3 million subscribers made it to the end of the video where he admits that YouTube already removed the copyright strikes against his account and informed him that his channel would not be deleted. But in a follow-up video titled “My Channel will NOT be deleted” posted on September 8th, Light thanked himself and “every single person that contributed” to the ongoing conversation about Remove Your Media unfairly threatening creators’ livelihoods. When I recently spoke with Light via email, he told me that over 60 of his videos had been temporarily taken down after he was hit with over a dozen copyright strikes stemming from Remove Your Media’s Digital Millennium Copyright Act (DMCA) takedown requests. Light said that “having a video taken down or struck is almost second nature” for people running reaction channels. But he insisted that the way Remove Your Media has been going after creators is abusive and unrelated to any legitimate interest in protecting copyrighted IP. “I think [Remove Your Media is] trying to get the attention of these companies, to hopefully get hired by them,” Light said. “But because they’re so sloppy, they’re damaging themselves. With all the major channels covering this, they’re doomed.” While it’s easy to get the sense that Light might have been playing up some of this drama for viewers’ attention, it is clear that a number of other content creators have similarly been hit with an inordinate amount of copyright strikes. Many of these creators specialize in anime and gaming reaction videos that feature blurred-out or otherwise altered pieces of copyrighted footage. The creators superimpose the altered copyrighted footage (like anime opening sequences or full episodes) onto videos of themselves reacting to and/or commenting on it. Though these types of videos are kind of like an inverse Mystery Science Theater 3000 where the on-camera creators are meant to be the focus, viewers can also use them as a (less-than-ideal) way to watch copyrighted material without having to pay for it. The creators insist that their content is fundamentally transformative and protected by fair use, but that has not kept YouTube from sending them warnings about their accounts potentially being erased. Remove Your Media feels it does not need to extensively review all of a creator’s videos to file DMCA takedowns in good faith Remove Your Media legal representative Evan F. Stone told me that Viz Media and Crunchyroll are two of the company’s “biggest anime clients.” Stone explained that Remove Your Media “reports thousands of allegedly infringing links every day” — some of which are “forwarded by the copyright owners themselves,” while others are discovered through online searches. Stone also noted that some clients are “very lenient” with certain types of content, despite their weak fair use defense. But he was adamant that the content being produced by YouTubers Remove Your Media has gone after is not transformative enough to fall under fair use. “Commentary, in this context, does not make the use transformative: these videos show the copyrighted work in its entirety, and a great deal of this content sits behind a paywall,” Stone said. “Add the fact that some viewers are watching these versions rather than the licensed versions, and the effect on the marketplace is real.” Stone also pointed out that some reaction video creators use their free YouTube channels as a way to encourage viewers to subscribe to their paid Patreon pages. In Remove Your Media’s view, when a reaction creator’s business model “is recording someone watching full episodes and selling those recordings, episode included, behind a Patreon paywall,” the company does not need to extensively review every single one of their uploads to file DMCA takedowns in good faith. “For any particular video a creator believes was noticed in error, the counter-notice exists for exactly that situation, and it works,” Stone said. “What [Remove Your Media] is seeing instead is channels countering every single video, full episode uploads included, swearing under penalty of perjury that each one was removed by mistake.” From Light’s perspective, channels like his are producing a unique kind of entertainment that also benefits copyright holders. “It’s free promotion for them at the end of the day,” he explains. Reaction videos of all genres have been popular on the internet for years, and it’s clear from view counts on YouTube that anime fans in particular are watching this kind of content. Light feels that because “the [anime industry] doesn’t do the best job promoting their works outside of Japan,” he and his peers have been picking up the advertising slack with their videos. And while Remove Your Media says that it works on behalf of certain anime distribution platforms including Crunchyroll and Viz Media, Light believes that “no company in the industry would want to align with a company so sloppy.” Crunchyroll did not respond to The Verge’s questions about whether it has hired Remove Your Media to enforce copyright and Viz Media would not comment on record. Similar to Light, Ron and Michelle Jackson — the couple behind the Rice & Ginger YouTube channel — uploaded a video last week announcing that their account might be deleted within a few days after being flagged hundreds of times for copyright violations. In addition to emphasizing that their video was not meant to be clickbait-y, the Jacksons encouraged viewers to subscribe to their Patreon page, and they said that the larger wave of creator takedowns is being caused by “one individual making hell for basically all of us.” The Jacksons did not respond to The Verge’s ask for comment about whether that unnamed individual might be Eric Green or Remove Your Media and how they were able to avoid having their channel deleted on the date (September 6th) listed in their first video addressing the situation. On September 9th, the YouTubers said that their account was now scheduled to be deleted the following day, and they described YouTube’s approach to enforcing copyright as a broken system that allows for AI-powered mass reporting. By September 10th, the Rice & Ginger YouTube channel was still online, and the Jacksons were back with a new video thanking Light for speaking out on their behalf and “saving” their channel. When I reached out to YouTube asking how it handles DMCA takedown disputes between reaction video creators and IP rightsholders, policy communications manager Jack Malon said that the company “does not decide who owns the rights to content.” Rather than mediating the legality of who owns the copyrighted content and whether a creator’s videos fall under fair use, YouTube brings the two parties together and leaves it to them to work the matter out between themselves. “When we receive valid legal requests, we remove the content to comply with copyright law,” Malon said. “If uploaders believe they have the right to use the material, including for exceptions like fair use, they can file a counter notification.” Malon added that YouTube only removes creators’ videos “when a rightsholder submits a valid and legal takedown request” because the company has to comply with copyright law. According to YouTube, creators can solve copyright strikes by submitting counter notifications — a process that involves providing evidence of ownership or permission to use all elements featured in the removed content. In response to my questions about how Remove Your Media selected the creators that were affected by the takedowns, he said that the company prioritized going after YouTube channels that generate “feeder traffic for paywalled copies of episodes nobody licensed to them.” “For any particular video a creator believes was noticed in error, the counter-notice exists for exactly that situation, and it works,” Stone said. “What [Remove Your Media] is seeing instead is channels countering every single video, full episode uploads included, swearing under penalty of perjury that each one was removed by mistake.” This drama highlights how certain dynamics of the creator economy may be changing Malon told me that, after a creator submits counter notifications, their videos can be restored and the strikes on their accounts can be resolved “if the rightsholder does not respond with proof of legal action to restrain the allegedly infringing content.” But it’s not entirely clear how YouTube confirms the validity of a claimant’s purported ownership of removed content. On September 10th, Light returned to his channel with another video lambasting both Remove Your Media and YouTube for the way the companies have gone about initiating and handling DMCA disputes. Light said that the Jacksons’ videos will be restored within 10 business days as a result of “all of the noise that [we] made together,” and he addressed people who have speculated that he might be leaning into this drama for financial gain. “It’s funny to me I start having problems with my AdSense the 10 days I choose to go after YouTube,” Light said, explaining that his videos have been demonetized during the DMCA fight. “Really makes you think. So maybe we should shut the fuck up when we say stupid shit like ‘he’s banking on the situation. He’s only doing this for money.’” Though Light and others who have spoken out about the way YouTube handles copyright issues might not be trying to capitalize on being deplatformed, this drama has highlighted how certain dynamics of the content creator economy may be changing. Anime reaction videos probably aren’t going away anytime soon, but it does seem like studios and distributors are becoming more aggressive about monitoring how their IP is being repurposed online. And while creators of all kinds still rely on fair use to produce the content that pays their bills, they may need to tread much more carefully if they want to avoid getting caught up in an aggressive purge. Meta says it’s changing AI suggestions after posing invasive personal questions
2026-09-11

Meta says it’s changing AI suggestions after posing invasive personal questions

The Verge | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
The change comes after a viral video showed Meta AI prompting a user to identify the child in one of her videos. The change comes after a viral video showed Meta AI prompting a user to identify the child in one of her videos. Meta says it’s making changes to the prompts suggested by its AI chatbot after a viral video showed it digging for personal information about a woman’s young daughters, as reported earlier by Futurism. In a statement to The Verge, Meta spokesperson Dina El-Kassaby says the company “missed the mark,” adding that “the feature never should have prompted the individual with questions like that.” Last week, Instagram user Kalie Robins posted a video explaining how Meta AI presented her with an invasive suggestion after cross-posting a clip to Facebook. The AI prompt, “Who is the child passenger?” appeared beneath a video of her and her child singing in the car. When she selected the prompt, Robins says Meta AI pieced together information about her daughters from her previous posts, as well as posts from her relatives. It suggested additional prompts asking about her children’s ages and where Robins lives with her family. Robins says Meta AI also showed photos of her daughters posted to the platform, including one she claims she deleted years ago. Meta has built its AI assistant into Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Messenger, where it can answer questions, write posts, and generate images. In July, the company pulled a feature that allowed people to create AI deepfakes of other Instagram users after facing fierce backlash. Meta’s El-Kassaby now says it has fixed the issue causing Meta AI to suggest prompts related to personal topics. “We created this feature to help people get more information about topics or posts that interest them,” El-Kassaby says. “When someone clicks on a prompt and uses this feature, the response it generates is based on information the user already has access to.” In this case, Meta AI may have pulled from videos where Robins named her children, according to El-Kassaby. New York City’s last pickpocket doesn’t need a smartphone
2026-09-11

New York City’s last pickpocket doesn’t need a smartphone

The Verge | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Reviews from TIFF 2026: All of a Sudden, The Only Living Pickpocket in New York, Rogue Trooper, How We Stand, and Below. Reviews from TIFF 2026: All of a Sudden, The Only Living Pickpocket in New York, Rogue Trooper, How We Stand, and Below. Hello once again from Toronto, where for a week every year I watch far too many movies and tell you all about them. The festival, better known as TIFF, is always a great preview of the months ahead in film, with a nice mix of mainstream fare, awards contenders, indie surprises, and genre flicks destined to be cult classics. The streamers also love to make their presence felt here, previewing some of their most anticipated projects. Last year, for instance, Netflix showed off both Guillermo del Toro’s long-awaited Frankenstein as well as the third Knives Out movie, and I was also able to watch everything from the terrifying video game adaptation Exit 8 to Park-Chan wook’s dark satire No Other Choice. This year looks similarly promising. There are new films from directors like Takashi Miike, Ryûsuke Hamaguchi, and Werner Herzog, and very intriguing features including a four-hour-long documentary on Elon Musk. There are also previews of a surprising number of Halloween-adjacent streaming series in the form of Netflix’s Below, Peacock’s Crystal Lake, and Amazon’s Carrie. I’m aiming to see just shy of 30 films and series over the next week or so, and, just like last year, I’ll be writing daily dispatches covering every single thing I see with a brief review. And things have gotten off to a very interesting start. On my first day I watched four movies and the premiere of a streaming series, highlighted by John Turturro’s wonderful turn as a technophobic petty thief in The Only Living Pickpocket in New York. I also saw one of the most Canadian things I’ve ever watched and a three-hour drama that somehow doesn’t feel overlong. Here, in no particular order, is what I’ve seen so far. In theaters on October 30thImagine if Uncut Gems was a little less stressful, but much more heart-wrenching. Harry (Turturro) is an aging pickpocket, one who sticks to what he knows while the world advances around him. He doesn’t understand smartphones, has no idea what USB means, and laments that no one carries cash anymore. But he’s still able to make a dishonest living and take care of his sick wife. Then one day he accidentally steals a crypto gizmo from the wrong guy, and is pulled into a tense thriller that puts everyone around him at risk. The film is a beautiful love letter to the city of New York, with lots of shots of busy streets and packed subways, but it’s mostly an incredible showcase for Turturro. He plays a genuinely bad guy, but you can’t help but love him in spite of it; at one point he literally steals candy from a child, and it made me smile. The final moments of the film are just about perfect. A story about conversation and connection, and one that — despite being over three hours long — absolutely breezes by, much like a great evening out where you suddenly realize it’s 2AM. Split between France and Japan, and helmed by Drive My Car director Ryusuke Hamaguchi, All of a Sudden comes to life after a chance meeting between Marie-Lou (Virginie Efira), the workaholic director of a nursing home, and Mari (Tao Okamoto), a playwright who is coping with a terminal cancer diagnosis. The two connect instantly and spend an entire night talking about everything from the ruinous impact of capitalism to the practical aspects of caring for elderly patients. It all feels natural and heartfelt, though, and at its core, All of a Sudden is a movie about what it means to be human: how we want to spend our time, and how we treat those around us, particularly those who are different or difficult. It sounds cliché, but it left me feeling both hopeful and terrified for the future. The film is also downright gorgeous, and there’s a good chance you’ll immediately start pricing tickets to Paris or Kyoto after you step out of the theater. Below is the rare story set in Newfoundland, on the east coast of Canada. It’s a compelling creature feature, a thriller where a mysterious monster is slowly terrorizing a remote fishing village that has been decimated by dwindling fish stocks. Josh Hartnett stars as a father of three who is just trying to survive, when strange chunks of ice start appearing in the harbor, portending something real bad. I watched the first two (of six) episodes, and even though it’s pretty clear what is going on, the show does a remarkable job of building up the mood and suspense, which makes the horrific reveals hit harder. Below is also surprisingly authentic and funny; anyone who has been to the East Coast will feel right at home when they see a bucket of salt beef or the dreaded screech. An animated satirical sci-fi movie based on the comic of the same name, Duncan Jones’ Rogue Trooper is set on a toxic planet that is home to a never-ending war that is a lucrative venture back on Earth. It follows a squad of genetically engineered supersoldiers who find themselves stranded in enemy territory, and their numbers steadily dwindle over the course of the film. Unfortunately, despite some occasionally beautiful sci-fi imagery that imbues a real sense of scale, Rogue Trooper mostly falls flat, with a drab story, forgettable characters, and action that lacks any real weight. Even worse, despite boasting a cast including Jemaine Clement, Matthew Berry, and Philomena Cunk herself, Diane Morgan, it’s also not funny at all. After moving to Los Angeles as part of the Indian Relocation Act in the 1950s, three sisters find themselves separated from each other after their mother is killed. The story then jumps forward a few decades, when each sister is living a very different life; one is trying to make it as an actress despite being typecast, one is a doctor after being adopted by a white family, and the youngest is struggling after passing through dozens of foster families. How We Stand explores the real impact of some truly horrific policies and programs, and the trio of actors at its core — Amber Midthunder, Alyssa Wapanatâhk, and Isabel DeRoy-Olson — are all excellent. But the film is saddled with writing that can feel too on the nose, and its magical realism elements feel underutilized until the very end. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Why Apple’s next computer should be for the smart home
2026-09-11

Why Apple’s next computer should be for the smart home

The Verge | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
After the “intelligent personal hub,” Apple needs to build the intelligent home hub. After the “intelligent personal hub,” Apple needs to build the intelligent home hub. John Ternus didn’t mention Apple Home once during his first keynote as Apple CEO. But he did open by describing an “intelligent personal hub” that prioritizes local processing, protects your privacy, and integrates with all your other devices, apps, and services. While Ternus was talking about the iPhone — not a new smart home device — what he spelled out is exactly what I want to see Apple make: an intelligent home hub, a computer for your home. Rumors suggest Apple’s first smart home hub with a screen is imminent. While that’s long overdue, it’s likely to be more interface than infrastructure, a device that can run your home locally but still uses the cloud. What the smart home needs is a computer to run it — one that controls and processes data from connected devices locally, improving speed, reliability, and privacy while securely storing your digital life, from photos and files to music and movies. Crucially, it would do all this inside your home. The cloud has worked well for years, but the recent proliferation of AI-powered home features, such as generating text descriptions from security camera footage, makes local computing more compelling. On-device processing can be faster and more private, and it depends less on an internet connection. These features will also evolve quickly to feed more context to our smart homes, bringing more utility but also a greater need for privacy and security. This type of dedicated home computer — or smart home brain — was an emerging category at last week’s IFA consumer tech show. Anker launched its Eufy MindBase home hub, and Ugreen debuted its HomeAgent, both combining network-attached storage, local AI to process and store footage from cameras, with a smart home hub to connect all your devices. But one piece is still missing: networking. A hub. AI. A router. Are you getting it? Call it an Apple Home Server. Most home routers haven’t caught up with today’s smart home. Despite years of talk about smarter routers, manufacturers and ISPs still seem reluctant to take on the complexity. Comcast has had Thread and Matter support in its gateways since 2022, but hasn’t turned them on. Connecting devices to your home network remains one of the most frustrating parts of the smart home, with manufacturers, ISPs, and platforms all pointing fingers when things fail. Ikea’s recent troubles rolling out new Matter-over-Thread devices are just one example. Here’s where Apple could step in and combine all three — a smart home hub, local storage with AI processing capabilities, and a home router — into one device. A hub. AI. A router. Are you getting it? Call it an Apple Home Server. There’s no evidence Apple is building such a thing. But the company’s recent contributions to OpenWrt, the open-source software that powers most routers, could be a tentative first step. One Apple box for your home: Connect it to your modem, and it becomes your Wi-Fi router, Matter Controller, Thread border router, smart home hub, private AI computer, and home server. It stores your files, photos, music, and movies; processes your camera footage locally; runs your automations even when the internet is down; and optionally backs everything up to iCloud. It wouldn’t be another smart home hub; it would be the computer your home needs. Plug it in, sign in with your Apple account, and it just works. That last part is crucial, and plays directly to Apple’s strength: hardware that is “super easy to use without a big learning curve,” as Ternus put it this week. Apple also has experience combining these pieces. AirPort Express put networking and media streaming on one box; AirPort Extreme and Time Capsule added NAS capabilities; and HomeKit Secure Routers brought Apple’s security into the network itself. Apple walked away from all these products in 2018, saying at the time it might return to the space if it found a meaningful way to progress routers further. The pieces have never fit together more perfectly than they do now. Apple is deeply involved in developing Matter and is spearheading the Thread networking protocol. An Apple Home Server could bring the entire smart home stack together, letting Apple control everything end to end and remove much of the complexity. Matter even has a blueprint for part of this device: the Matter Home Router Access Point device type combines Wi-Fi networking and Thread Border Router functionality, and Apple’s contributions to OpenWrt focus on HRAP implementation. According to reporting by Matter Alpha, Apple’s code includes production-grade networking infrastructure that could be turned into a consumer product. There’s no evidence that’s what Apple is doing; it could simply be for router manufacturers. But the pieces are falling into place. With Apple bringing Apple Intelligence to its HomeKit Secure Video service in iOS 27, a Home Server device could take that service to the next level with fully local processing. Local is the key. An Apple Home Server would be a supercharged Apple Home Hub, powering local-first smart home automations, keeping data private and secure at home, and improving responsiveness for devices like smart lights, locks, and security cameras. It could work alongside existing home hubs — the HomePod, Apple TV, and rumored HomePad — for voice and screen interfaces. Apple loves subscriptions. But it loves its premium hardware even more. This might go against the grain. Apple loves subscriptions. But it loves its premium hardware even more, and this would undoubtedly be premium hardware. Also, a device like this would give Apple a powerful foundation for everything it does in the home: iPhones, Macs, iPads, Apple TVs, HomePods, its existing digital services, and potential future AI-powered ones. And, unlike a typical smart home hub, it could appeal to anyone who wants a reliable, private home router and a way to store their digital life at home. Smart home infrastructure is broken, and Apple is uniquely positioned to fix it. An Apple Home Server is the hardware backbone Apple Home has always needed. As our digital lives move deeper into the cloud and AI makes the smart home more dependent on data, the case for a computer to run our homes gets stronger, and building one on open standards could limit lock-in. Apple has all the pieces. The smart home has been waiting for someone to put them together. If Apple finally builds it, the only surprise will be that it took this long. Meet the under-35s shaping the future of biotech
2026-09-11

Meet the under-35s shaping the future of biotech

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Every year, MIT Technology Review puts together a list of some of the brightest and best young minds working across science and technology. Our 35 Innovators Under 35 are the ones to watch—people whose research and technical work stands to shape the future of their fields. This year, the list includes nine people who are transforming biotech. And this week, I’m going to give you a taste of some of the very cool stuff five of them are working on, which includes lifesaving innovations and groundbreaking “age reversal” tech. Let’s start with Paschal Kija, a 28-year-old who has developed a device to treat postpartum hemorrhage—a dangerous birth complication that contributes to around 29% of maternal deaths in his home country, Tanzania. The Mkanda Salama (“Safe Wrap” in Swahili) is easy to use and costs just $70. A study found that it stopped postpartum bleeding in 73% of women within 20 minutes. 2. Making brain electrodes inspired by Japanese art For decades, scientists have been developing, testing, and implanting brain electrodes. These devices are literally inserted into people’s brains, so while they can help us understand brain activity and treat various neurological disorders, it’s not totally surprising that they can also cause a bit of damage. Xiao Yang, 34, is working on ultra-small electrodes, which she hopes will have less of an impact on surrounding brain tissue. Her electrodes are flexible, too—in fact, they look a lot like actual neurons. Yang is also creating sheets of electrodes to study brain cells in the lab. Inspired by kirigami—the traditional Japanese art of cutting paper to form three-dimensional shapes—she’s created a sheet of electrodes with a honeycombed structure shaped like a spiral basket. And she’s already using it to study brain cells. 3. Developing an all-new treatment for baby KJ In 2024, Kyle “KJ” Muldoon Jr. was born with a rare and potentially fatal genetic disorder. Sarah Grandinette was a member of a team that developed an entirely new, personalized treatment for him—a gene-editing therapy essentially designed to correct a genetic misspelling. Grandinette, who is now 26, created cells with KJ’s genetic variant and used them to screen gene-editing approaches; then she tested potential medicines in mice and monkeys. KJ ultimately got his first dose of the resulting treatment when he was about seven months old. He responded well and was eventually discharged from hospital. He’s “doing pretty great,” she says. 4. Reversing the aging process to treat eye disease The buzziest tech in longevity right now centers on reprogramming—attempts to rewind the age of cells by resetting them to a more embryonic-like state. In a study published in 2020, Yuancheng (Ryan) Lu (now 34) and his colleagues showed that a reprogramming therapy reversed vision loss in aged, blind mice. Now an almost identical version of that therapy is being tested in people with eye disease. Life Biosciences, the company developing the drug, dosed its first volunteer in June. Last year, Samuel King used a generative AI model to come up with new genetic blueprints for bacteriophages—teeny viruses that can infect bacteria. Once he had those blueprints, he printed them out as strands of DNA. In experiments, he found that those AI-designed viruses could create new copies of themselves, burst out of bacterial cells, and infect other nearby bacteria. Viruses aren’t alive, but King, 27, hopes that AI-designed life forms might one day be used to make drugs or soak up pollution. You can read more about these innovators, and the others on the biotech list, here. This article first appeared in The Checkup, MIT Technology Review’s weekly biotech newsletter. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. The state’s effort to expand the “right to try” is making headway, and the first drugs are about to be reviewed. Discussions of the life stage are often clouded by misinformation. Kidneys kept at subzero temperatures in pressure-controlled containers can be stored for days before transplantation, raising hopes for longer-term storage of donated human organs. When Eli Regalado first heard God speak to him, he wondered whether he was hallucinating. According to Eli and his wife, Kaitlyn, He told them to get married, buy a house, and start having kids. Then in 2021, divine guidance steered them in an unexpected new direction: crypto. That October, the Regalados later testified in court, they received holdings in a little-known digital coin. “Take this to my people for a wealth transfer,” Eli heard God say. Over time, they came to believe that He wanted them to launch their own coin. The Regalados created INDXcoin, which they promoted through family, friends, and contacts in evangelical Christian circles. In all, more than 500 people handed over more than $3 million. But within a year, the project collapsed. Investors lost it all, leaving many to wonder where the funds went and whether they had fallen victim to an elaborate fraud. Scientists have spent half a century exploring whether we could counteract climate change by releasing reflective particles into the stratosphere, mimicking the cooling effects of volcanic eruptions. But even after hundreds of studies, we still don’t know how well it would work or what else it might do—and there’s no systematic plan for clearing up that uncertainty. Reflective, a research organization, has now attempted to fill that gap. The San Francisco nonprofit has published a detailed road map of the experiments, studies, and infrastructure that it says would be needed to make informed decisions about the use of solar geoengineering, MIT Technology Review can reveal. Find out what it would take to make informed decisions about solar geoengineering. Throughout the history of the computer chip, engineers have treated waste heat as an inevitable cost of a calculation. Hannah Earley, however, thinks it’s a design choice. Earley, 31, is cofounder and CTO of Vaire Computing, which builds chips that recycle energy usually thrown away as heat, a strategy known as reversible computing. The approach could make data centers (and our laptops and phones) much more energy efficient. Last year, Vaire announced a key breakthrough: a chip with a resonator that recovered more energy than it lost, even after the energy needed to power the component was taken into account. Here’s how she plans to bring an old idea about energy-efficient computers into the future. Hannah Earley is one of the computing and robotics honorees on our 35 Innovators Under 35 list for 2026. Reducing reliance on any single source of crucial energy technology makes sense. But the tension raises a broader question for me: how much should countries take advantage of cheap, available tech, and how much should they cut themselves off from foreign sources to develop their own, even if it costs more? In the past, getting to the North Pole involved a treacherous trip through ice many meters thick. But last year, a research vessel encountered open water and thin ice, which created an easy passage. It provided a reminder of how quickly the Arctic is changing. Now scientists are digging deep below the seabed to find out if the Arctic Ocean was ever ice-free—and what that could mean for the future of Earth’s northernmost waters. MIT Technology Review
Powering AI is an architecture problem
2026-09-10

Powering AI is an architecture problem

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
On July 22, 2026, a transmission line fault in Ashburn, Virginia—the heart of the world's largest data center cluster—knocked more than 3 gigawatts of load off the grid in seconds. And it wasn't the first time. Two years earlier, a single failed surge arrester dropped roughly 60 Virginia facilities and 1,500 megawatts at once. No one could anticipate so much uniform load responding to grid faults the same way, at the same time. The AI power debate is mostly about generation: more turbines, more solar, more transmission. The grid needs more electrons. But the outages in Virginia weren't supply failures; they were architecture failures. And a giant wave of interconnections is arriving on that same architecture, putting grid reliability at risk. It's a problem nobody wants to own. The grid was built around predictable loads: steel mills, refineries, and houses at dinnertime. Different load sizes, same process—drawing power smoothly, misbehaving occasionally, and recovering gracefully. But AI data centers don't behave that way. An AI campus can swing 70% of its load in milliseconds during a training run, then trip offline just as fast at the first sign of trouble upstream to protect billions in compute. Each is rational alone. Together, at gigawatt scale, they're a problem the grid has never solved—and the next wave of data center campuses is planned at exactly that scale. The standard data center power stack hasn't changed in decades. Medium-voltage power arrives, transformers step it down, low-voltage uninterruptible power supply (UPS) units condition it, and it reaches the racks. Push that design to AI scale, and it cracks in three places. First, the UPS sits deep inside the building, close to the racks. But its batteries are an undersized spare tire, designed to handle an outage for a few minutes, not to absorb load swings this fast and volatile around the clock. Second, the UPS spends most of its life in bypass. Legacy converters waste enough power that operators run in eco-mode: A static switch feeds the racks directly from the grid and nothing filters in either direction. The compute's swings go out raw, and grid transients—sub-millisecond events that can damage or take down equipment—come in too fast for any switch to catch. Third, the protection logic was written when "large load" meant 50 megawatts. This protection logic can't see the grid it is now a part of, so when trouble hits upstream, it does exactly the wrong thing: it drops out. In the 2024 Virginia event, most of the lost load traced to protection schemes that count voltage dips and disconnect on the third one—as designed, at the worst moment. This isn't sloppy engineering. It's careful engineering the load has outgrown. Move it up—from 480 volts to medium voltage (13.8 kilovolts and higher), the voltage large sites draw from the grid. Move it out—from the data hall to modular enclosures near the substation so the building holds only compute and the cooling that keeps it alive. Move it into the path—instead of a battery that watches and reacts, a system every electron runs through, all the time. There's nothing to detect and nothing to switch because nothing was ever routed around it. On paper, three straightforward upgrades. In practice, they rewrite every line item downstream. When thousands of GPUs spin up together, the system absorbs the swing and hands the grid a flat load profile. When a disturbance hits, the equipment behind it never notices. A difficult neighbor becomes a predictable one. And when the utility needs help, it becomes a useful one. Interconnection changes, too. The utility certifies one medium-voltage box instead of untangling every transformer, UPS, chiller, pump, and switchgear lineup behind it. Engineers swap chip generations without a fresh interconnection study. Months come off the permitting timeline. Inside the fence, UPS rooms become compute or cooling space. Density per construction dollar climbs. And the economics flip. Equipment that runs at medium voltage, sits outside, and stores its own energy can qualify for tax credits, and earn revenue in grid programs like peak shaving and demand response. Backup power stops being insurance and starts paying for itself. In early 2026, we tested a full-scale system at the National Laboratory of the Rockies, a U.S. Department of Energy facility and the only place in the Western Hemisphere that can replicate real grid faults and AI-scale load swings concurrently in the same loop. We hit it from both directions: real AI load profiles hit the compute side at full medium voltage. Grid faults hit the utility side, including a full zero-voltage event. The compute side didn't flinch. Neither did the grid side. It cleared the large-load voltage ride-through requirements from the Electric Reliability Council of Texas (ERCOT), the grid operator, with room to spare. Those rules exist because operators no longer take facilities this size on faith, and more are coming. Most of the industry treats them as hurdles. A medium-voltage, inline system clears them out of the box. Compliance isn't an added feature. It's what the architecture does. Much of what looks like a grid problem in the AI buildout sits inside the fence, in equipment sized for a load that no longer exists. Move the right pieces up, out, and into the path, and a grid liability becomes a grid asset. Density goes up. Permitting time comes down. Backup power earns its keep. The engineering works—and the next wave of AI factories is being built on it. The industry hasn't named this layer yet. We call it the medium-voltage AI UPS. The name matters less than the choice: those factories can arrive as a strain on the grid or as strength for it. We already know how to build the second kind. This content was produced by ON.energy. It was not written by MIT Technology Review’s editorial staff. It makes it easy to trick them into doing things they shouldn’t, such as telling you how to sabotage an aircraft’s navigation system. AI doesn’t just learn stereotypes from its training. It can cook up new ones, too. The misbehavior is called reward hacking. This is what you need to know. In a new interview, the billionaire philanthropist sounds an alarm on the urgency of getting our AI policies in order. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. MIT Technology Review
This road map could help us decide whether to deploy solar geoengineering
2026-09-10

This road map could help us decide whether to deploy solar geoengineering

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
A San Francisco nonprofit has published a detailed road map of the experiments, studies, and infrastructure that it says would be needed to make informed decisions about the use of solar geoengineering, MIT Technology Review can reveal. Scientists have now spent half a century exploring the possibility that we could counteract climate change by releasing reflective particles into the stratosphere, mimicking the cooling effects of volcanic eruptions. But even after at least hundreds of studies on the concept, known as stratospheric aerosol injection (SAI), big gaps remain in the scientific understanding of how well it would work and what else it might do—and there has been no systematic plan for clearing up that uncertainty. Reflective, a research organization that funds studies on solar geoengineering, has today attempted to fill that gap with the release of its SAI Research Roadmap. “Our mission is to equip the world with the data and tools required for informed decision-making about sunlight reflection fast enough to matter,” says Dakota Gruener, the organization’s cofounder and chief executive. “Our sense is the world may need to make very consequential decisions on timelines far shorter than our research system is prepared for.” The hope is the exercise will guide scientific efforts and encourage philanthropies or government agencies to fund high-priority work and “responsibly accelerate research,” says Gruener. If all the work is done in a coordinated way, it would take about a decade and cost around $370 million—and if it’s not, it would require roughly 20 years and nearly $1.4 billion, the report estimates. While Gruener stresses that Reflective doesn’t advocate using this form of solar geoengineering, the report does make the case for conducting outdoor experiments, which would release successively larger amounts of sulfur dioxide (or materials that would convert into it) in the stratosphere to observe what happens. That is a controversial standpoint. Since 2002, hundreds of academics have signed an open letter calling for a ban on outdoor experiments and an "international non-use agreement," arguing that such a powerful technology could never be governed in a globally equitable way. And some signatories argue that more studies can never address one of the biggest questions about using solar geoengineering: Who gets to do it. “The first-order questions, from my perspective, are not technical,” Aarti Gupta, co-initiator of the non-use initiative and professor of global environmental governance at Wageningen University in the Netherlands, told me in a recent on-stage interview. “The core question is: Who would control a planet-altering technology like stratospheric aerosol injection? Who would develop it, and who would deploy it, and to what end? To serve what purposes, and whose purposes? Those questions are very fundamental, because this planet-altering technology will have winners and losers.” Since Gruener incorporated Reflective in late 2023, the nonprofit has quickly become an important  player in solar geoengineering research. It has now raised more than $20 million from a number of prominent charities and individuals, and it’s provided around $4 million to several dozen research groups. Reflective has also undertaken a handful of its own projects to promote research, including the development of an open-source solar geoengineering simulator and an online hub for collaborative research. Earlier this year, Reflective released its SAI Uncertainties database, which identified a long list of scientific unknowns and  engineering obstacles that would need to be addressed before even a small-scale solar geoengineering effort could move ahead. (I wrote about the specific scenario and the unknowns in this earlier piece.) Some of the biggest uncertainties involve what gas or particles would make the most sense to use and what would happen once they were released in the dry stratosphere. It’s not clear, for example, whether they’d spread out in a way that maximizes the reflectivity—or clump together and quickly fall out into the troposphere, the lowest layer of Earth’s atmosphere. The road map builds upon the database, highlighting the path to addressing most of those questions. The initial phase in Reflective’s road map, labeled “foundational knowledge,” includes additional computer simulation studies and lab experiments designed to shed light on the potential impacts on different regions, ecosystems, and phenomena, including ocean circulation patterns, ice sheets, and crop yields. The report also notes the need to begin developing more observational tools during this phase to improve understanding of the baseline conditions of the stratosphere—and, in turn, our ability to assess any effects from the eventual release of materials. This first stage would last two to three years and cost $30 million to $75 million, though some of the analysis and observational work would continue into subsequent phases. The next stage would include using modified aircraft to release 10 metric tons of sulfur dioxide into the stratosphere, four times over the course of two seasons. The full research stage could take four to eight years and cost $70 million to $150 million, the report says. The work during it may reduce uncertainty about the “cooling efficacy” of solar geoengineering, or how much the planet would cool per ton of sulfur released, by about 25%. The experiments during the next phase would step those levels up dramatically, releasing 25,000 tons of sulfur dioxide over the course of one season, at least once but possibly twice. That research stage, which includes other work as well, would last four to 11 years, run $270 million to $1.1 billion, and decrease efficacy uncertainty by around 66%, according to the road map. The final phase of research would be ongoing monitoring of full-scale solar geoengineering, if the world goes ahead with it. The goal would be to gather real-life data on the technology in action, update estimates of the effects in models, and spot any “unexpected or undesired consequences.” Gruener says that the road map is intended as a Version 1, meant to be “concrete enough for people to argue with.” But Reflective intends to update the plan as it receives additional reactions from researchers and other observers, and it will invite such feedback through a mechanism on the site. She also notes that there are firm “stage gates,” set up between the latter stages—in other words, research shouldn’t proceed to the next phase if the experiments suggest that the releases don’t have the hoped-for impact, show worrisome downsides, or fail to resolve crucial uncertainties. “Our road map has these gates precisely because there may be points where the answer is ‘You should stop,’” she says. Most observers I spoke to about the report agree that these studies could reduce uncertainty about the effectiveness of solar geoengineering and our technical ability to carry it out. But highlighting the scientific importance of outdoor experiments won’t necessarily make them any easier to move ahead with. Several earlier proposals to carry out such experiments, including Harvard’s SCoPEx and the UK-based SPICE project, were ultimately halted amid opposition from environmentalists or policymakers. In addition, not everyone agrees that experiments at those scales will get us to the point where we’re capable of making an “informed decision.” Wil Burns, a research professor and legal scholar at American University and a signatory to the International Non-Use Agreement, fears that scientists won’t be able to understand the extent of the potential downsides, including impacts on the protective ozone layer and changes to regional precipitation patterns, until we’re carrying out full-fledged solar geoengineering. “The research would give you some answers,” he says. “I just don’t think it gives you answers that are that relevant. To get to those relevant answers, you have to deploy at scale—and I just don’t think that’s ever tenable.” That’s because, in his view, using the technology would violate principles of intergenerational equity: If the world continues emitting greenhouse gases, increased levels of solar geoengineering would merely mask the continued warming of the planet. Burns says that means future generations—people who had no say in its use—couldn’t turn it off without triggering a sudden surge of warming, known as termination shock. “What that would do, in my mind, is put a sword of Damocles over future generations,” he says. “So even if you could, quote-unquote, ‘prove it works,’ I don’t think from an intergenerational perspective it would ever be tenable.” (Some researchers, however, have argued that the risks of termination shock are less likely than often assumed—and that solar geoengineering could be slowly dialed down over time.) Ilan Gur, the former CEO of the Advanced Research and Invention Agency (ARIA), the UK research department that funded 21 geoengineering research projects last year, applauds Reflective’s road map. “Whether you’re a scientist or a policymaker or just a concerned citizen, our goal should be as quickly and efficiently as possible to answer the biggest questions scientifically that would tell us [whether] this is an approach that might work or that would never work,” he says. “We should all want to spend the effort and money to buy down that uncertainty, so my view is 100% the approach that Reflective is taking is the right one.” Sebastian Eastham, an associate professor in sustainable aviation at Imperial College London who is leading an ARIA-funded research project exploring another approach to engineered cooling, agrees that the outdoor experiments described in the Reflective road map can’t resolve all the unknowns. But he says the map helps begin a conversation about how to make decisions concerning the use of a tool with potential benefits and risks, in the face of escalating climate dangers. “Every hard decision that has ever been taken has been in the context of unresolved uncertainty,” he says. “That’s just the nature of things.” Eastham adds that it’s become essential to move beyond computer simulations to address some of the key questions, arguing that appropriately designed and executed outdoor experiments can teach us so much more than millions of hours of computational processing time “that it almost becomes irresponsible to say, ‘Well, there cannot be ever any experiment.’” The risk is “that we spin our wheels running the same computational simulations over and over and over again,” he says. That could prevent researchers from learning essential things about the effectiveness or the dangers of stratospheric aerosol injection. Gruener says the risks that solar geoengineering could exacerbate inequality need to be considered, but notes that unchecked warming also threatens to disproportionately harm developing regions. She also acknowledges that outdoor experiments won’t fully address the scientific unknowns but stresses that they can answer a lot—and carry little environmental risk. She notes that 10 tons of sulfur dioxide is less than 2% of the amount that the global aviation industry releases into the atmosphere each day. “Some people will be uncomfortable with any discussion of any outdoor experiment, but if we want decisions made on good science … then these are questions that an experiment will be necessary to address,” Gruener says. She fears that the rising dangers of climate change will put growing pressure on nations and other actors to move forward with solar geoengineering, even if no one has done the necessary research to reduce scientific uncertainty and sort out the technical challenges. “We don’t think the alternative is decisions not happening at all,” she says. “We think the alternative is decisions being made in a panic or on lack of evidence.” A key tax credit in the US ended, but heat pump sales are still going strong. Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged. El Niño? Climate change? All of the above? MIT Technology Review
Can the US battery market untangle from China?
2026-09-10

Can the US battery market untangle from China?

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
The US is hitting records for the rapid growth of its energy storage market. That’ll go a long way to shoring up the grid, increasing reliability and also cutting emissions, since batteries can help store energy from intermittent renewables like wind and solar. Crucially, this is all happening with the help of cheap Chinese batteries, though there’s been a concerted effort to reduce the US’s reliance on them. Most recently, in an executive order in late August, the Trump administration declared a national emergency that essentially bans Chinese batteries from being used in grid-scale energy storage systems. There’s an argument to be made about reducing reliance on any single source of a crucial energy technology. But all this tension raises a broader question for me: How much should countries take advantage of cheap, available tech, versus cutting off major sources to force development of their own factories even if that comes at a higher cost? This is hardly America’s first push to move away from Chinese influence in the battery supply chain. One of the major policy tools used in recent years is restricting the tax credits designed to incentivize use of the new technologies. Limiting the types of projects that are eligible can help reduce the cost of local technologies so they’re more competitive with otherwise cheaper imported options. Back in 2022, the US government designed the tax credits that were part of the Inflation Reduction Act to restrict where a battery’s minerals could be mined, processed, or recycled, as well as where a battery and its components were assembled. Those tax credits underwent a makeover in 2025, but the Trump administration has taken a similar tack. New legislation requires that starting in 2026, 55% of the cost of materials used for new energy storage projects must come from outside China and other restricted countries or the projects won’t qualify for tax credits. And we can’t forget about tariffs. Import taxes for batteries increased to 25% in January, up from 7.5%. But the new executive order is a more drastic move. It bans the installation of “any foreign-produced bulk-power system electric equipment” that poses a national security risk. The order specifically calls out battery energy storage systems, as well as inverters and transformers. “An outright ban was a bit of a surprise, and it does create a bit of concern for domestic players in the US,” says Shan Tomouk, energy storage and energy lead for Benchmark Mineral Intelligence, an energy industry analyst. The move is likely to slow deployment of grid-connected energy storage projects in the near term, according to analysis from BloombergNEF, an energy consultancy. Projects could face delays as developers wait for clarity on the rules. Depending on the detailed guidance from the Department of Energy, which is expected by the end of the year, some projects may need to find alternative sources for their cells, whether they’re domestically produced or imported from other countries. These will likely be more expensive than Chinese imports, says Isshu Kikuma, an energy storage analyst at BloombergNEF. “Worst case, those projects could get canceled,” he says. Technically, the order applies even to existing energy storage plants, though it’s unlikely that they’ll be taken offline because of their batteries’ origin. Since most of these plants currently use Chinese batteries, enforcing the order to the letter would essentially mean removing most installed battery energy storage from the US grid, Kikuma says. In the longer term, the US will eventually be able to meet its own demand for batteries. The country could have enough capacity by about 2030, though some factories may not ramp up or run at their full capability, meaning domestic supply won’t actually meet demand until later in the 2030s. New factories from LG Energy Solutions, Samsung SDI, Ford, and SK On are set to come online or ramp up by next year. In an ironic twist, a slowing EV market is helping, as some factories originally designed for vehicle batteries are retooling to build cells for grid storage instead. But it will come at a cost. Today, batteries produced in the US are still significantly more expensive than those made in China. Even switching to imports from other countries like South Korea would likely be more expensive. This is a crucial issue that goes beyond the US and even beyond batteries. China is miles ahead of much of the rest of the world on technologies like solar panels and batteries. Through years of government support and experience with research and manufacturing, the nation is an energy powerhouse. There’s a delicate political balance to maintain as the world figures out how to navigate this situation. There’s cheap technology on offer, which can help drastically reduce emissions and energy costs. But there can be risks associated with relying too much on any one player for crucial technologies. The UAE plans to invest €40bn in Germany, including 1GW of data centres

The UAE plans to invest €40bn in Germany, including 1GW of data centres

The Next Web | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
German Chancellor Friedrich Merz and UAE President Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan at the Chancellery in Berlin on 10 September 2026 The United Arab Emirates plans to invest €40bn ($46.4bn) in Germany, including in new data centres with a combined capacity of about one gigawatt. The two countries announced the package in a joint declaration on 10 September. UAE President Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan was on a state visit to Germany, where he met Chancellor Friedrich Merz. The package includes “investments in advanced digital infrastructure, including the development of new state-of-the-art data centres with a capacity of approximately 1 GW,” the declaration says. Germany committed to “fostering a favourable environment for their implementation”. Hugh Leask reported the plan for CNBC. The visit ran from 9 to 11 September, at the invitation of the German president, Frank-Walter Steinmeier. It was the first state visit to Germany by a UAE president, according to the declaration. Both sides expect the package to strengthen industrial competitiveness, advance technological leadership and support long-term growth in Germany, the text says. Merz and Sheikh Mohamed also set up a Strategic Dialogue. It is meant to revive the comprehensive strategic partnership the two countries founded in 2004. The declaration does not say which companies will build the data centres, where they will be or when. AFP and Investment Monitor also noted the missing details. The German government’s own summary describes the package as investment in digital infrastructure and new data centres. It gives no capacity figure. The two sides also signed a declaration of intent to set up a German-UAE Investment Council. It is meant to advance investment and strengthen ties between the public and private sectors. Companies from both countries signed 29 memorandums of understanding and agreements, worth more than €9.356bn in total, according to the declaration. CNBC, citing reports, said about €10bn would go to Bavaria. The UAE state news agency WAM said Sheikh Mohamed met Bavaria’s minister-president, Markus Söder, in Munich. According to WAM, the UAE pledged €10bn for Bavaria, as part of the €40bn package. The money covers industry, advanced technology, AI, data centres, digital infrastructure and energy security, WAM reported. The UAE’s Ministry of Foreign Trade and the Bavarian government exchanged a declaration of intent on strategic investment. The two countries signed a separate declaration of intent on data. It covers “the exploration of cooperation on Data Hosting and Information systems through the establishment of Data Embassies,” the joint declaration says. According to the text, the cooperation will explore “a new model of technology diplomacy through secure and resilient digital infrastructure”. Both sides also committed to expanding cooperation in artificial intelligence and emerging technologies. That includes exchange, investment, research and joint development, according to the declaration. Outside the €40bn package, the leaders welcomed further plans involving Covestro, RWE, ADNOC and Masdar. They build on existing UAE investments, including the energy group XRG’s investment of about €15bn in the German chemicals maker Covestro, according to the declaration. Energy cooperation covers LNG, renewables and hydrogen. The two sides signed letters of intent on exploring defence cooperation and on police and security cooperation. They agreed to take defence ties “beyond a supplier-and-recipient dynamic”, the declaration says. Non-oil trade between the two countries reached $15.5bn in 2025, up more than 14% on 2024. Investment flows between them exceeded $10bn from 2021 to 2025, according to the declaration. CNBC reported that the UAE has already invested about €34bn in Germany, including in chemicals and renewable energy. The UAE is negotiating a free trade agreement with the EU. Germany supports “a comprehensive, commercially ambitious agreement”, the declaration says. “The UAE invests for the long term and builds partnerships that endure,” said Sultan Ahmed Al Jaber, the UAE’s industry minister, according to AFP. The UAE’s foreign trade minister, Thani Al Zeyoudi, spoke to Bloomberg TV in Berlin on Thursday. He said the UAE is “very keen” to connect its economy, trade and investments with economic hubs around the world. Paul Musgrave is an associate professor of government at Georgetown University in Qatar. He told CNBC that the UAE, like all Gulf countries, wants to diversify its overseas portfolio and build an economy beyond oil. Abu Dhabi is also looking at investments in supply chains and corporations for strategic purposes, he said, “including, of course, developing new friends and influence in Europe”. He also described the benefit for Berlin. “Germany is in a bit of an economic doldrums right now, it needs to have something that Chancellor Merz can deliver as a win,” Musgrave said. The UAE’s AI spending reaches well beyond Germany. Abu Dhabi says its financial centre holds $100bn of AI capital. G42, the Abu Dhabi AI group, has given up Huawei and let Washington monitor its data centres. At home, the UAE is splitting up its $30bn AI data centre after Iran named it as a target. The joint declaration condemned Iran’s attacks on the UAE and its de facto closure of the Strait of Hormuz. In Germany, the energy company Uniper announced a €5bn data centre plan in August. The EU’s €20bn plan for five AI gigafactories ran into delays in June. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

A Texas judge has found TikTok liable for misleading parents about child safety
2026-09-11

A Texas judge has found TikTok liable for misleading parents about child safety

The Next Web | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
A Texas judge ruled on Thursday that TikTok broke state law by telling parents its app was safe for their children while knowing that it was not. District Judge Cory Liu found that the company had intentionally misled the public about its safety standards, and he decided it on summary judgment, meaning he did not think the question needed a jury. ikTok promoted Restricted Mode as the setting that would keep graphic material involving drugs, alcohol, nudity, and violence away from a child’s feed. Texas showed the court that children could still reach that material with the setting switched on. The state also pointed to how TikTok sorted content internally: some things that broke its own community guidelines were filed as “hard to find” rather than “do not allow”, which left them on the platform and reachable, just less visible. Attorney General Ken Paxton, whose office brought the case in January 2025, said TikTok “sacrificed the safety and innocence of children for engagement and numbers”. The claims ran on two tracks: the SCOPE Act, the Texas law on children and online platforms, and the state’s general consumer protection statute, which is the one that turns a marketing promise into a legal obligation. What makes this different from the last two years of child safety litigation is the shape of the outcome. These cases almost always end in a payment with no admission attached. TikTok has never reached a jury, settling three more teen addiction cases only recently. It paid $400mn to the Justice Department over children’s privacy. Meta agreed to pay up to $16.68bn to settle with 29 states. In each of those, the company wrote a cheque, and the question of whether it had actually done anything wrong went unanswered. Here it has been answered. Liability is established. The trial scheduled for next month is not about whether TikTok deceived anyone but about what it owes for having done so, which covers civil penalties, statutory damages and a permanent injunction that could force changes to how the app works in Texas. A settlement now would be a settlement on top of a finding, not instead of one. TikTok has not accepted any of this. In earlier filings, it argued that the SCOPE Act claims rest on a law that is unconstitutional and that federal children’s privacy rules override the state’s, according to reporting by the legal outlet MLex on those filings. An appeal is widely expected. Texas has become the busiest state in this area by some distance. The same office has sued Netflix over alleged surveillance and addictive design, and it extracted more than $1bn from Meta in a separate settlement. Florida has its own action against TikTok under its child social media law, and California has just banned personalised feeds for under-16s. The pressure is arriving from several directions at once, and it is no longer only about money. The ruling also lands while the broader argument is being tested in front of juries elsewhere. Meta is in court in the biggest consumer protection case in the country, where opening statements put the sum at issue at $193bn. What Texas has shown is that a state does not need to wait for a jury to reach the liability question, provided the paper trail is clear enough. Judge Liu decided this one on the documents. The detail worth holding onto is the filing system. A platform that labels rule-breaking content “hard to find” has not removed it, and a parental control that hides material rather than blocking it is doing something different from what the marketing says. That gap between the promise and the switch is what the court decided was deceptive, and it is a gap that exists in more products than this one. Tencent-backed chipmaker Enflame nearly triples in its Shanghai debut
2026-09-11

Tencent-backed chipmaker Enflame nearly triples in its Shanghai debut

The Next Web | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Shares in Shanghai Enflame Technology closed 179% above their offer price on their first day of trading on Shanghai’s Star Market on Friday. The Tencent-backed AI chipmaker had raised about 6.12bn yuan ($911m) in its initial public offering, Bloomberg reported. The stock opened at 410 yuan, against an IPO price of 142.18 yuan. It rose as much as 234% to 475 yuan during the day before closing at 397 yuan, Ann Cao reported for the South China Morning Post. That gave Enflame a market value of 170.9bn yuan ($25.5bn), according to the SCMP. Reports published during the session gave other figures: CNBC put the gain at 206%, and Business Insider at about 200%. The wider market fell. The Star 50 Index dropped 1% on Friday and the benchmark CSI 300 Index 0.8%, the SCMP reported. Retail investors bid for 4,073 times the shares available to them, according to Enflame’s filing to the exchange, cited by the SCMP. About 7 million online investors ordered 42.1bn shares. Individual investors got an allocation rate of 0.025%, one of the lowest in mainland China this year. The robot maker Unitree had a rate of 0.018%, the SCMP reported. CNBC gave a higher figure. It reported that the retail offering drew orders for more than 6,000 times the shares available, before Enflame reallocated more stock to that group. Enflame sold 43 million shares in the IPO, according to Bloomberg. It won approval for the listing in June. Enflame was founded in Shanghai in 2018 and makes AI chips for training and inference. It has fewer than 900 employees and has never turned a profit, Diana Li reported for Bloomberg. The company takes its name from a Chinese mythical figure credited with inventing fire. Its co-founders, Zhao Lidong and Zhang Yalin, both worked at AMD. Zhao spent two decades in Silicon Valley and was a vice president at the state-backed Tsinghua Unigroup. Zhang rose through AMD’s Shanghai research centre to run its core chip development, according to Bloomberg. From the start, the founders chose not to compete directly with Nvidia’s general-purpose graphics processors. They built a different type of chip, designed to work outside Nvidia’s ecosystem, Bloomberg reported. The share rise brought each founder’s fortune to $2.5bn, according to Bloomberg. Both are bound by long lockup periods and must meet certain targets before they can sell. Enflame did not respond to Bloomberg’s requests for comment. Tencent holds a 20% stake in Enflame and is also its largest customer. Sales to Tencent made up 84% of Enflame’s revenue in 2025, up from about 38% a year earlier, according to Bloomberg. The chips run in large data centres, powering chatbots, recommendation systems and generative AI. Revenue rose to 990m yuan in 2025, from 722m yuan in 2024, CNBC reported. Enflame posted a net loss of 1.16bn yuan for 2025, narrower than the 1.5bn yuan loss the year before, according to Bloomberg. The company forecast a loss of no more than 860m yuan for the first nine months of 2026. Bloomberg reported that revenue for the period is expected to more than triple from a year earlier. Enflame expects to break even in 2026 or 2027, Business Insider reported. Enflame plans to spend the proceeds on developing its fifth- and sixth-generation AI chips, according to CNBC. It wants them to match the performance of high-end products from international rivals. “The appeal of the company lies in its position as one of China’s leading cloud AI chipmakers, as well as Tencent’s dual role as both a key customer and major shareholder,” analysts at Huajin Securities, including Li Hui, wrote in a note, Bloomberg reported. They added that Enflame trails its peers in revenue scale and gross margin. Paul Triolo, a partner and head of technology at the advisory firm DGA, called Enflame’s choice of alternative chips a “high-risk form of strategic independence”. He told Bloomberg that the approach “may be good for Tencent and municipal data centers”. It could be “less attractive in the open developer market”, he said. Enflame still relies on overseas manufacturers to produce its advanced chips, Qianer Liu reported for The Information. In late 2023, geopolitical tensions forced it to downgrade some chip designs to keep access to TSMC’s factories, according to Bloomberg. To bring in revenue, it has relied on Chinese state-backed computing projects in cities such as Wuxi and Qingyang. Enflame is the last of China’s “four little dragons” of AI chipmaking to list. The other three are Moore Threads, MetaX and Biren. MetaX soared nearly 700% on its first day of trading in December, CNBC reported. Moore Threads rose 425% on its debut the same month, according to Bloomberg, and has since planned a Hong Kong listing. Biren jumped 76% on its listing in January. CNBC and Bloomberg described those shares differently. According to CNBC, all three have remained above their listing prices. Bloomberg reported that they have all fallen back substantially. Moore Threads is down more than 60% from its record high, and MetaX and Biren more than 40% from their peaks, according to Bloomberg. Other Chinese chip listings have also surged on debut. In July, the memory maker CXMT rose nearly 466% on its Shanghai debut, CNBC reported. International chipmakers led by Nvidia held nearly 60% of China’s AI accelerator market in 2025. CNBC cited that IDC figure from Enflame’s prospectus. US export controls and Beijing’s lukewarm interest in importing advanced chips have shut off Nvidia’s exports to China’s data centre market, according to CNBC. Z.ai said its GLM-5.3-Flash model runs entirely on China-made chips. Analysts said it likely used chips from Huawei, Enflame and other local companies, CNBC reported. China is “still at a very early stage of a multi-year IPO upcycle”, Morgan Stanley analysts wrote in a note on 28 August, Business Insider reported. Many of the new listings are developing technologies China sees as strategically important, though many are not yet profitable, the bank said. “Capital markets are therefore critical to help them scale up,” its analysts wrote. Goldman Sachs expects China’s semiconductor capital spending to reach $82bn by 2030, according to CNBC. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Amazon lets its advertisers buy ads in ChatGPT in a pilot with OpenAI
2026-09-11

Amazon lets its advertisers buy ads in ChatGPT in a pilot with OpenAI

The Next Web | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Amazon will let advertisers buy ads in ChatGPT through its own advertising platform. Amazon Ads announced the partnership with OpenAI in a blog post on 10 September. Select US advertisers are already testing it in a pilot. Advertisers who plan and run campaigns with Amazon Ads can now extend them to ChatGPT, the company said. Annie Palmer reported the deal for CNBC, and Catherine Perloff for The Information. Brands buy the placements through Amazon DSP, Amazon’s demand-side platform for programmatic ads. They do not have to deal with OpenAI directly, TechRadar reported. Advertisers can pay per click or per thousand impressions. Amazon sets up and manages the campaigns, an Amazon spokesperson told CNBC. OpenAI’s ad system makes every decision about which ads are delivered. The ads appear as text or images beneath ChatGPT’s answers. OpenAI has said its ads sit below the end of a response and are “clearly labeled” as sponsored. CNBC gave an example. A user asking ChatGPT for marathon training tips could see an ad for running shoes sold on Amazon. Chris Conetta is director of omnichannel supply at Amazon DSP. “Conversational ads represent the fastest growing engagement opportunity for brands to reach new and existing audiences,” he said in the announcement. With access to ChatGPT Ads, he added, advertisers can extend their campaigns to where customers spend time in conversational chat platforms. Delta Vacations, the airline’s holiday packages business, is one of the brands in the trial. “We’re proud to be among the first brands testing this new approach,” said its president, Katrin Koenig. She said the company would use “deep consumer insights” to decide how and when Delta Vacations appears in ChatGPT ads. Amazon Ads sells programmatic advertising across Amazon’s own properties and the open internet. The company says the platform draws on “trillions of proprietary browsing, shopping, and streaming signals”. It says it delivers results without relying on ad identifiers. OpenAI began selling sponsored placements in ChatGPT in the US in February, CNBC reported. Its first advertisers included the retailers Best Buy and Williams-Sonoma. OpenAI said in February that ChatGPT had 900 million weekly active users. At the end of August, OpenAI said the ad business had reached a $1bn annualised revenue run rate. In August, OpenAI said it would bring ChatGPT ads to 31 European markets. Until now, advertisers bought ChatGPT ads through OpenAI’s own interface or through smaller ad-tech partners such as Criteo and Kargo, The Information reported. In April, TNW reported that OpenAI was hiring ad-tech firms to build conversational ad formats. Amazon’s ad business is far larger. TechRadar, citing Marketing Dive, described it as the third-largest digital advertising business, with about $70bn in annual ad revenue. Amazon has heavily restricted access to its own store from AI platforms such as ChatGPT, Anthropic’s Claude and Google’s Gemini, CNBC reported. It has invested in its own AI shopping tools instead. In August, a US court let Perplexity’s AI agent shop on Amazon, overturning a ban. Amazon has also been a ChatGPT advertiser itself. It began buying ads on ChatGPT in recent months, according to CNBC. Data from the market intelligence firm Sensor Tower show it was the top retail advertiser on the platform between April and August. Other companies are building shopping into ChatGPT too. Synchrony, the card issuer, wants its Amazon and Walmart store cards to work inside ChatGPT. The two companies already have a financial tie. Amazon agreed in February to invest $50bn in OpenAI, starting with $15bn, according to Amazon. The rest, $35bn, depended on conditions being met. The Information has reported that Amazon completed that second tranche. Under the same partnership, Amazon said OpenAI’s offerings would be available through its Bedrock cloud service and would use its Trainium chips.
